| 29 | **1296** | **G23**; Split cards into groups of fixed size k with consecutive values: ordered frequencies, repeatedly start at smallest unassigned value; O(n log n) with TreeMap. **Recognition:** To form fixed-length groups of consecutive integers, the smallest remaining value must start a group; consume its necessary successors. | BASE: unlike G22 variable-length chain-extension, fixed group length forces consuming a specific k-value run from current minimum. |
| **G24 — Fill lexicographically best prefix under global sum: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 30 | **1663** | **G24**; Lexicographically smallest lowercase string of fixed length and numeric value: start with all a, distribute required extra score right-to-left; O(n). **Recognition:** To minimize a string while preserving target alphabet score, leave early positions at minimum value and allocate extra score from the right. | BASE: postpone expensive letters to the latest positions to preserve smallest earlier prefix. |
| **G25 — Descending-rank positional insertion: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 31 | **406** | **G25**; Reconstruct queue from height and in-front-taller count: sort taller first (ties by k ascending), insert each at index k; O(n²) shifting. **Recognition:** Sort people by descending height and insert each at the position equal to its count of preceding taller/equal people. | BASE: earlier inserted people are all tall enough to count toward the next person’s k, so local insertion preserves constraints. |
| **G26 — Keep maximum cost per equal-run: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 32 | **1578** | **G26**; Minimize cost to remove characters so adjacent characters differ: in each maximal equal-character run delete all but maximum-cost character; O(n). **Recognition:** When neighboring characters must differ, within each original equal-character run retain the most expensive element and delete the rest. | BASE: independent runs and keep-max argument minimize deletion expense. |
| **G27 — Deadline-count earliest-threat elimination: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 33 | **1921** | **G27**; Eliminate maximum monsters before any reaches city: integer arrival deadline ceil(distance/speed), sort deadlines, eliminate one per minute; O(n log n). **Recognition:** When one operation per minute eliminates one enemy, sort each enemy’s arrival deadline and process the earliest deadline first. | BASE: earliest-deadline-first prevents avoidable losses; do not conflate with interval overlap or heap scheduling. |

**Family structure:** 27 algorithm families / 33 LeetCode questions. A new family resets the code invariant. The sequence within each family is explicitly foundation → meaningful variant when a nonredundant variant exists. No implied evolution between families.

### T10 recognition cues and BASIC Java 21 templates

These are deliberately **small invariant sketches**, not complete submissions; local variables/inputs represent a generic method context, and placeholder comments require the concrete question-specific implementation. Present a family’s template exactly once when beginning its first question. `java.util.*` imports are assumed.

**G1 — Farthest reachable frontier & minimum jumps.** **Clue:** Jump lengths allow covering a forward interval; greedily retain the farthest reachable endpoint.

```java
int farthest = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < a.length && i <= farthest; i++) {
    farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + a[i]);
    // Reachability or layer-boundary logic depends on objective.
}
```

**G2 — Unlimited local profit exchanges.** **Clue:** Independent positive adjacent increases can be collected without compromising later trades.

```java
long profit = 0;
for (int i = 1; i < prices.length; i++)
    profit += Math.max(0, prices[i] - prices[i - 1]);
```

**G3 — Global balance and feasible starting point.** **Clue:** A circular route is possible when global supply covers demand; negative running balance invalidates an entire candidate prefix.

```java
long total = 0, tank = 0;
int candidate = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    long delta = gain[i] - cost[i];
    total += delta; tank += delta;
    if (tank < 0) { candidate = i + 1; tank = 0; }
}
```

**G4 — Ascending-capacity greedy matching.** **Clue:** Each smallest available demand should consume the smallest sufficient resource to leave larger resources for later demands.

```java
Arrays.sort(needs); Arrays.sort(resources);
int i = 0, j = 0, matched = 0;
while (i < needs.length && j < resources.length) {
    if (resources[j] >= needs[i]) { matched++; i++; }
    j++;
}
```

**G5 — Two-end capacity packing.** **Clue:** Maximize two-item containers by pairing heaviest remaining item with lightest possible partner.

```java
Arrays.sort(weights);
int left = 0, right = weights.length - 1, boats = 0;
while (left <= right) {
    if (left < right && weights[left] + weights[right] <= limit) left++;
    right--; boats++;
}
```

**G6 — Descending marginal reward under capacity.** **Clue:** When units have independent constant value by category, consume highest-value categories first.

```java
Arrays.sort(items, (a,b) -> Integer.compare(b[1], a[1]));
long answer = 0;
for (int[] item : items) {
    int take = Math.min(capacity, item[0]);
    answer += (long)take * item[1]; capacity -= take;
    if (capacity == 0) break;
}
```

**G7 — Smallest completion deficit first.** **Clue:** Sort independent completion/purchase costs ascending and spend budget on cheapest attainable units first.

```java
Arrays.sort(costs);
int completed = 0;
for (int cost : costs) {
    if (cost > budget) break;
    budget -= cost; completed++;
}
```

**G8 — Sort by differential assignment gain.** **Clue:** With an exact quota for category A, sort jobs by (costA-costB) and assign those most advantageous to A.

```java
Arrays.sort(jobs, (x,y) -> Long.compare((long)x[0]-x[1], (long)y[0]-y[1]));
long total = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < jobs.length; i++)
    total += (i < quotaA) ? jobs[i][0] : jobs[i][1];
```

**G9 — Offline queries with sorted prefix optimum.** **Clue:** Sort records by threshold and build the best score observed up to each threshold for many independent queries.

```java
Arrays.sort(records, Comparator.comparingInt(x -> x[0]));
int best = 0;
for (int[] record : records) {
    best = Math.max(best, record[1]);
    // Answer compatible queries by sweeping sorted limits or binary-searching prefix maxima.
}
```

**G10 — Local spacing with occupancy constraints.** **Clue:** A chosen location forbids adjacent locations; choose earliest legal spot when choices do not harm later feasibility.

```java
for (int i = 0; i < places.length; i++) {
    boolean leftFree = i == 0 || places[i-1] == 0;
    boolean rightFree = i+1 == places.length || places[i+1] == 0;
    if (places[i] == 0 && leftFree && rightFree) {
        places[i] = 1; planted++;
    }
}
```

**G11 — Denomination-preserving change allocation.** **Clue:** Maintain inventory of small denominations; use larger change combinations only when they leave sufficient future change.

```java
int small = 0, medium = 0;
for (int paid : payments) {
    if (paid == base) small++;
    else if (paid == 2*base) { if (small == 0) break; small--; medium++; }
    else { /* preferentially spend one medium + one small; else three small */ }
}
```

**G12 — Last-occurrence partition closure.** **Clue:** Close a partition only once every symbol encountered so far has its final occurrence inside it.

```java
int[] last = new int[26];
for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) last[s.charAt(i)-'a'] = i;
int start = 0, end = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
    end = Math.max(end, last[s.charAt(i)-'a']);
    if (i == end) { /* emit partition [start,end] */ start = i+1; }
}
```

**G13 — Earliest valid cut for distinct-character pieces.** **Clue:** When a new character repeats within the current segment, start a fresh segment immediately.

```java
boolean[] seen = new boolean[26];
int parts = 1;
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    int idx = c-'a';
    if (seen[idx]) { Arrays.fill(seen, false); parts++; }
    seen[idx] = true;
}
```

**G14 — Bounded consecutive character-run construction.** **Clue:** Construct a string from remaining character counts while forbidding three identical consecutive outputs.

```java
StringBuilder out = new StringBuilder();
while (remainingA > 0 || remainingB > 0) {
    // Choose preferred available char unless it creates a forbidden run.
    // If preferred char is blocked, choose the alternate or terminate.
}
```

**G15 — Feasible open-count interval.** **Clue:** Wildcard parentheses may behave as open, close, or empty; maintain minimum/maximum feasible unmatched opens.

```java
int low = 0, high = 0;
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    if (c == '(') { low++; high++; }
    else if (c == ')') { low = Math.max(0,low-1); high--; }
    else { low = Math.max(0,low-1); high++; }
    if (high < 0) { /* impossible */ }
}
```

**G16 — Two-direction flexible bracket feasibility.** **Clue:** Locked parentheses constrain fixed symbols; unlocked positions may become either bracket but cannot disappear.

```java
int balance = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
    // Treat unlocked positions as potential '('; reject impossible closes.
}
for (int i = s.length()-1; i >= 0; i--) {
    // Treat unlocked positions as potential ')'; reject impossible opens.
}
```

**G17 — Bidirectional local lower-bound constraints.** **Clue:** Each index must exceed neighbor allocations when its rating exceeds neighbor ratings; combine minimal directional bounds.

```java
int[] left = new int[n], right = new int[n];
Arrays.fill(left, 1); Arrays.fill(right, 1);
for (int i=1;i<n;i++) if (rating[i]>rating[i-1]) left[i]=left[i-1]+1;
for (int i=n-2;i>=0;i--) if (rating[i]>rating[i+1]) right[i]=right[i+1]+1;
long sum=0;
for (int i=0;i<n;i++) sum+=Math.max(left[i],right[i]);
```

**G18 — Sort-first sign flip optimization.** **Clue:** Flip negative values before nonnegative values; after negatives exhausted, leftover parity determines whether smallest absolute value flips.

```java
Arrays.sort(a);
for (int i = 0; i < a.length && k > 0 && a[i] < 0; i++, k--) a[i] = -a[i];
// If k is odd, subtract twice the smallest absolute value from sum.

```

**G19 — Reverse-operations minimization.** **Clue:** Work backward from target using inverse actions; divide when possible because halving dominates repeated decrement/increment.

```java
while (target > start && doubles > 0) {
    if ((target & 1) == 0) { target /= 2; doubles--; }
    else target++;
}
// Finish with unit steps once doubling resource or useful reductions are exhausted.

```

**G20 — Suffix-digit monotonic correction.** **Clue:** To get the largest number not exceeding n with nondecreasing decimal digits, repair any descending adjacent pair by reducing the left digit and filling suffix with 9.

```java
char[] d = Integer.toString(n).toCharArray();
int cut = d.length;
for (int i = d.length-1; i>0; i--)
    if (d[i-1]>d[i]) { d[i-1]--; cut=i; }
for (int i=cut;i<d.length;i++) d[i]='9';
```

**G21 — Rightmost-beneficial digit exchange.** **Clue:** Maximize a number with at most one swap by bringing the largest later digit into the earliest improvable position.

```java
int[] last = new int[10];
for (int i=0;i<digits.length;i++) last[digits[i]-'0']=i;
for (int i=0;i<digits.length;i++) {
    for (int want=9; want>digits[i]-'0'; want--)
        if (last[want]>i) { /* swap current with rightmost desired digit; return */ }
}
```

**G22 — Extend existing consecutive chains first.** **Clue:** When choosing between extending a shorter already-open consecutive subsequence and starting a new one, extend an existing chain first.

```java
Map<Integer,Integer> remaining = new HashMap<>(), tails = new HashMap<>();
for (int x : sorted) {
    if (remaining.getOrDefault(x,0)==0) continue;
    // Prefer consuming an open chain ending at x-1.
    // Otherwise require available x+1 and x+2 to open a length-3 chain.
}
```

**G23 — Smallest remaining consecutive-start exhaustion.** **Clue:** To form fixed-length groups of consecutive integers, the smallest remaining value must start a group; consume its necessary successors.

```java
TreeMap<Integer,Integer> freq = new TreeMap<>();
for (int x : a) freq.merge(x,1,Integer::sum);
while (!freq.isEmpty()) {
    int smallest = freq.firstKey();
    for (int d=0;d<groupSize;d++) {
        // remove one occurrence of smallest+d or reject.
    }
}
```

**G24 — Fill lexicographically best prefix under global sum.** **Clue:** To minimize a string while preserving target alphabet score, leave early positions at minimum value and allocate extra score from the right.

```java
char[] ans = new char[length]; Arrays.fill(ans,'a');
int extra = target - length;
for (int i=length-1; i>=0 && extra>0; i--) {
    int add = Math.min(25,extra); ans[i] += add; extra-=add;
}
```

**G25 — Descending-rank positional insertion.** **Clue:** Sort people by descending height and insert each at the position equal to its count of preceding taller/equal people.

```java
Arrays.sort(people, (a,b) -> a[0] == b[0] ? Integer.compare(a[1],b[1]) : Integer.compare(b[0],a[0]));
List<int[]> placed = new ArrayList<>();
for (int[] person : people) placed.add(person[1],person);
```

**G26 — Keep maximum cost per equal-run.** **Clue:** When neighboring characters must differ, within each original equal-character run retain the most expensive element and delete the rest.

```java
int i = 0; long deleted = 0;
while (i < s.length()) {
    int j=i, total=0, keep=0;
    while (j<s.length() && s.charAt(j)==s.charAt(i)) {
        total+=cost[j]; keep=Math.max(keep,cost[j]); j++;
    }
    deleted += total-keep; i=j;
}
```

**G27 — Deadline-count earliest-threat elimination.** **Clue:** When one operation per minute eliminates one enemy, sort each enemy’s arrival deadline and process the earliest deadline first.

```java
Arrays.sort(deadline);
for (int minute=0; minute<deadline.length; minute++) {
    if (deadline[minute] <= minute) { /* cannot eliminate this one in time */ }
}
```

## C11. T11 KADANE & SUBARRAY DP — COMPLETE ORDERED QUESTION PLAN (LOCKED)

**Scope:** **14 globally unique LeetCode questions in 10 algorithm families (K1–K10)**. **Progress:** 0/14; **next:** LC **53**. The primary algorithm is a constant-state transition over **contiguous** segments: extend a segment ending at the preceding position or restart at the current position. This topic includes max/min sums, one-deletion and product/sign extensions, circular and repeated sequences, constrained consecutive runs and one advanced character-pair variant. A solution that searches arbitrary subsequences, partitions into many independently optimized groups, or maintains a general DP table belongs to T25 instead. Templates below are **basic invariant sketches only**, never LeetCode submissions. Each family begins afresh unless an explicit implementation-level extension is documented.

### T11 locked ordered question registry

| Seq | LC | Family; recognition clue; concept and canonical optimized Java 21 strategy | EXACT implementation change **within family** |
|---:|---:|---|---|
| **K1 — Extend-or-restart maximum sum / transformed scores: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 01 | **53** | K1; select one nonempty contiguous interval maximizing total, possibly with negative elements. One-pass Kadane: `ending=max(x,ending+x)`; retain best, O(n)/O(1). | **BASE:** one state for best nonempty subarray **ending here**, global maximum over endpoints. |
| 02 | **2606** | K1; contiguous substring where letters carry signed custom costs, empty substring allowed. Build 26-entry character cost lookup (default alphabet positions, override supplied letters), feed mapped score to zero-clamped Kadane; O(n+26). | Replace raw integer `x` with mapped character cost; initialize `ending=best=0` and clamp at 0 because empty output is allowed. |
| 03 | **2321** | K1; exchanging exactly one corresponding contiguous segment between two same-length arrays maximizes one final array sum. Compute both original totals and run zero-clamped Kadane on per-index differences `b[i]-a[i]` and `a[i]-b[i]`; maximize original total + attainable gain. | Replace character-cost mapping with a pairwise difference stream; apply the same gain tracker in **both directions** and add each best gain to its own original total. |
| **K2 — Dual maximum/minimum Kadane and circular complement: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 04 | **1749** | K2; maximize absolute value of a contiguous subarray sum with empty selection allowed. Track best positive ending sum and worst negative ending sum, maximize `max(maxSum,-minSum)`; O(n)/O(1). | **BASE:** simultaneous maximum/minimum extend-or-restart states; an absolute objective needs both signs. |
| 05 | **918** | K2; maximum **nonempty** circular contiguous sum. Track normal max/min Kadane and total sum; result `max(maxSum,total-minSum)` unless every value is negative, when return maxSum; O(n)/O(1). | Retain dual max/min ending states; add total and `total-minSum` wraparound candidate, plus all-negative guard to exclude empty wrapped selection. |
| **K3 — Repeated-array crossing optimum: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 06 | **1191** | K3; find maximum possibly-empty subarray across `k` concatenated copies without constructing them. Scan one copy for total, max prefix, max suffix, best one-copy Kadane; for `k>=2` compare single-copy best with `suffix+prefix+max(0,total)*(k-2)`; apply modulo only to final nonnegative answer. | **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY:** use prefix/suffix crossing decomposition and repeat-count algebra rather than the circular complement invariant of K2. |
| **K4 — Optional single deletion in nonempty best subarray: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 07 | **1186** | K4; maximum sum of a nonempty contiguous choice after deleting at most one element inside it. Two ending states `keep` (no deletion) and `drop` (one deletion used); update `drop=max(oldKeep,oldDrop+x)` before replacing keep; O(n)/O(1). | **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY:** introduce two coupled DP states distinguished by deletion budget; carefully disallow empty final segment. |
| **K5 — Max/min contiguous product: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 08 | **152** | K5; maximize nonempty contiguous product when negatives flip sign and zeros reset. Track maximum and minimum products ending at position; compute both from previous states and current value; O(n)/O(1). | **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY:** sum-based extend-or-restart alone is invalid under sign flipping; pair max/min **product** states using previous snapshot. |
| **K6 — Positive/negative product parity lengths: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 09 | **1567** | K6; maximize length of a nonempty contiguous segment with positive product, not the product magnitude. Track longest positive-product and negative-product lengths ending here; zero resets both, negative swaps role. O(n)/O(1). | **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY:** use sign-parity **length** states, not max/min product magnitudes; related sign transitions but not a direct code evolution from K5. |
| **K7 — Count qualifying consecutive suffixes: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 10 | **413** | K7; count contiguous arithmetic segments of length at least 3. Track number of qualifying arithmetic segments ending at current position: on equal adjacent differences increment `ending`, else reset, add to total; O(n)/O(1). | **BASE:** local adjacency condition determines how many *new* qualifying subarrays end here; accumulate suffix counts, not max score. |
| 11 | **2110** | K7; count all contiguous downward-by-exactly-one runs including single-element periods. Track length `run=previousRun+1` on difference -1, otherwise 1; add `run` to long total; O(n)/O(1). | Keep adjacency-driven ending-count and accumulated total; change predicate to `prev-current==1`, change minimum valid length from 3 to 1, and change reset state from 0 to 1. |
| **K8 — Alternating adjacent-comparison states: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 12 | **978** | K8; longest contiguous segment whose comparison signs alternate strictly. Maintain up/down lengths ending here; positive/negative neighboring difference extends opposite sign's run, equality resets both; O(n)/O(1). | **BASE:** two competing local trend states, extension permitted only when comparison direction flips. |
| **K9 — Single-rise-then-fall contiguous shape: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 13 | **845** | K9; longest contiguous strict mountain with at least one ascent then descent. Track increasing-run and descending-run lengths, invalidate flat/equal steps, and update answer only when both sides exist; O(n)/O(1). | **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY:** unlike indefinitely alternating slopes in K8, enforce a **single** rising phase followed by a falling phase; use up/down mountain lengths rather than two alternating-direction endings. |
| **K10 — Constrained character-pair Kadane: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 14 | **2272** | K10; maximum difference between counts of two distinct letters inside a contiguous substring containing **both** letters. For each ordered pair `(major,minor)`, scan the string, accumulate +1/-1 for those letters, only score when `minor>0`, and restart when the running count goes negative **and future minor occurrences remain**; O(26²n) time/O(26) frequency. | **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY:** pair-constrained Kadane needs presence tracking and remaining-minor feasibility; ordinary sum Kadane may choose an invalid substring without the negative letter. |

**Family count:** 10 (K1–K10); **14 questions**. K3–K6 and K8–K10 deliberately start new families; similar contiguous wording alone is not enough to connect implementations. K7 explicitly distinguishes *counting ending-here segments* from *optimizing a score*. Consider `long` for totals and transformed sums where constraints warrant it; apply modulo only where specified.

### T11 recognition cues and BASIC Java 21 templates

**K1 — Extend or restart the current maximum-score segment.** Clue: one contiguous segment, additive per-position score; the choice at a position is start here or extend the best ending just before it.

```java
long ending = a[0], best = a[0];  // nonempty variant
for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
    ending = Math.max((long) a[i], ending + a[i]);
    best = Math.max(best, ending);
}
// If empty is allowed, initialize ending=best=0 and use max(0,ending+score).
```

**K2 — Max and min ending sums.** Clue: absolute value or circular complement requires **both** largest and smallest contiguous totals.

```java
long maxEnd = a[0], minEnd = a[0];
long maxSum = a[0], minSum = a[0], total = a[0];
for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
    long x = a[i];
    maxEnd = Math.max(x, maxEnd + x);
    minEnd = Math.min(x, minEnd + x);
    maxSum = Math.max(maxSum, maxEnd);
    minSum = Math.min(minSum, minEnd);
    total += x;
}
// For circular maximum: use total-minSum only if maxSum >= 0.
```

**K3 — Prefix/suffix crossing on repeated copies.** Clue: the input array is virtually concatenated many times; optimal segment may span complete middle copies.

```java
long prefix = 0, maxPrefix = 0, total = 0;
for (int x : a) { total += x; maxPrefix = Math.max(maxPrefix, total); }
long running = 0, maxSuffix = 0;
for (int i = a.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    running += a[i];
    maxSuffix = Math.max(maxSuffix, running);
}
// Compare best single-copy Kadane with crossing copies.
// For >=2 copies, crossing = maxSuffix+maxPrefix+max(0,total)*(k-2).
```

**K4 — Two states for an optional deletion.** Clue: an otherwise contiguous maximum may skip at most one interior element; distinguish whether deletion was consumed.

```java
long keep = a[0], drop = Long.MIN_VALUE / 4, best = a[0];
for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
    long x = a[i];
    long nextDrop = Math.max(keep, drop + x);
    long nextKeep = Math.max(x, keep + x);
    keep = nextKeep; drop = nextDrop;
    best = Math.max(best, Math.max(keep, drop));
}
// drop means an actual element has been removed; final segment remains nonempty.
```

**K5 — Positive/negative product extremes.** Clue: negative multiplication swaps best and worst, so retain both ending products.

```java
long high = a[0], low = a[0], best = a[0];
for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
    long x = a[i], p = high * x, q = low * x;
    high = Math.max(x, Math.max(p, q));
    low = Math.min(x, Math.min(p, q));
    best = Math.max(best, high);
}
// Product width must follow problem constraints; zero naturally restarts states.
```

**K6 — Product-sign parity lengths.** Clue: optimize length under a positive-product requirement, not product magnitude.

```java
int positive = 0, negative = 0, best = 0;
for (int x : a) {
    if (x == 0) { positive = 0; negative = 0; }
    else if (x > 0) { positive++; negative = negative == 0 ? 0 : negative + 1; }
    else {
        int oldPositive = positive, oldNegative = negative;
        positive = oldNegative == 0 ? 0 : oldNegative + 1;
        negative = oldPositive + 1;
    }
    best = Math.max(best, positive);
}
```

**K7 — Ending-here count from local adjacency.** Clue: count every qualifying contiguous run, using how many valid segments end at each new position.

```java
long total = 0;
long ending = 0;   // for minimum length 3 arithmetic subarrays
for (int i = 2; i < a.length; i++) {
    if ((long)a[i] - a[i - 1] == (long)a[i - 1] - a[i - 2]) ending++;
    else ending = 0;
    total += ending;
}
// For singletons and strictly -1-adjacent runs, initialize/reset ending to 1.
```

**K8 — Alternating comparison lengths.** Clue: each adjacent strict comparison must be the opposite of the previous one.

```java
int up = 1, down = 1, best = a.length == 0 ? 0 : 1;
for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
    if (a[i] > a[i - 1]) { up = down + 1; down = 1; }
    else if (a[i] < a[i - 1]) { down = up + 1; up = 1; }
    else { up = 1; down = 1; }
    best = Math.max(best, Math.max(up, down));
}
```

**K9 — Strict mountain phases.** Clue: contiguous values must first strictly rise and then strictly fall, with both phases present.

```java
int up = 0, down = 0, best = 0;
for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
    if (a[i] == a[i-1] || (down > 0 && a[i] > a[i-1])) { up = 0; down = 0; }
    if (a[i] > a[i-1]) up++;
    if (a[i] < a[i-1] && up > 0) down++;
    if (up > 0 && down > 0) best = Math.max(best, up + down + 1);
}
```

**K10 — Ordered-character-pair constrained Kadane.** Clue: a substring score is a difference in character frequencies and **both** chosen characters must occur.

```java
for (int major = 0; major < 26; major++) for (int minor = 0; minor < 26; minor++) {
    if (major == minor) continue;
    int majorCount = 0, minorCount = 0;
    int remainingMinor = frequency[minor]; // precomputed on full string
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
        int c = s.charAt(i) - 'a';
        if (c == major) majorCount++;
        if (c == minor) { minorCount++; remainingMinor--; }
        if (minorCount > 0) { /* candidate variance majorCount-minorCount */ }
        if (majorCount < minorCount && remainingMinor > 0) {
            majorCount = 0; minorCount = 0;
        }
    }
}
```

### T12 recognition rules and BASIC Java 21 starter templates

Each template illustrates the base invariant only. Show once at its family’s first practice question, before that question’s optimized full solution. Methods named in comments or placeholders are problem-specific.

**ST1 — Delimiter validation, depth and nested scores.** **Recognition:** Use when nested opening/closing tokens must be checked, stripped, counted, or evaluated by depth.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Character> open = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    if (c == '(') open.push(c);
    else if (c == ')') {
        if (open.isEmpty()) { /* invalid close */ }
        else open.pop();
    }
}
// Open frames track nesting; adapt to depth, output, or score.
```

**ST2 — Minimum edits to balance parentheses.** **Recognition:** Use when invalid parentheses must be counted, removed, or repaired under explicit pairing rules.

```java
int balance = 0, invalidClose = 0;
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    if (c == '(') balance++;
    else if (c == ')') {
        if (balance > 0) balance--;
        else invalidClose++;
    }
}
// Unclosed opens and unmatched closes define the repair budget.
```

**ST3 — Balanced bracket swaps.** **Recognition:** Use when the string has equal counts but arbitrary order and swaps, not insertions/deletions, restore validity.

```java
int open = 0, swaps = 0;
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    if (c == '[') open++;
    else if (open > 0) open--;
    else { swaps++; open = 1; } // arbitrary swaps, equal counts assumed
}
```

**ST4 — Adjacent stack cancellation and grouped runs.** **Recognition:** Use when an incoming symbol may annihilate the latest retained symbol, possibly using counts.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Character> kept = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    if (!kept.isEmpty() && kept.peek() == c) kept.pop();
    else kept.push(c);
}
// In variants, change cancellation predicate or stack entry to (symbol,count).
```

**ST5 — Explicit delete-marker reduction.** **Recognition:** Use when each special marker unconditionally removes one preceding retained character.

```java
StringBuilder kept = new StringBuilder();
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
    if (c == '*') kept.setLength(kept.length() - 1); // guaranteed valid
    else kept.append(c);
}
```

**ST6 — Stack-based canonical paths.** **Recognition:** Use when separators and parent-directory tokens require undoing the last path component.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<String> dirs = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (String part : path.split("/")) {
    if (part.isEmpty() || part.equals(".")) continue;
    if (part.equals("..")) { if (!dirs.isEmpty()) dirs.removeLast(); }
    else dirs.addLast(part);
}
// Reconstruct absolute path from surviving components.
```

**ST7 — Validating external stack operation sequences.** **Recognition:** Use when a push order is fixed and a target pop order must be simulated.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> stack = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
int out = 0;
for (int x : pushed) {
    stack.push(x);
    while (!stack.isEmpty() && out < popped.length
           && stack.peek() == popped[out]) { stack.pop(); out++; }
}
// Valid exactly when out == popped.length.
```

**ST8 — Augmented minimum stack.** **Recognition:** Use when push/pop/top and minimum-of-current-stack must all be O(1).

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> values = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> mins = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
// On push x: values.push(x); mins.push(min(x, previousMin));
// On pop: pop both; min is mins.peek().
```

**ST9 — Lazy bulk updates on a bounded stack.** **Recognition:** Use when incrementing the bottom k entries must be fast without touching every element.

```java
int[] value = new int[capacity], pending = new int[capacity];
int size = 0;
// increment(k,delta): pending[Math.min(k,size)-1] += delta if nonempty
// pop(): transfer pending[top] to pending[top-1], return value[top]+pending[top]
```

**ST10 — Frequency-priority stack.** **Recognition:** Use when pop must choose most-frequent value, breaking ties by most recent insertion.

```java
java.util.Map<Integer,Integer> freq = new java.util.HashMap<>();
java.util.Map<Integer,java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer>> byFreq = new java.util.HashMap<>();
int highestFreq = 0;
// push x: increment freq[x], push x onto byFreq[newFreq], track highestFreq
// pop: remove from byFreq[highestFreq], decrease its freq and highestFreq if empty
```

**ST11 — Reverse Polish expression evaluation.** **Recognition:** Use when every operator follows its operands and no precedence/parentheses parser is needed.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (String token : tokens) {
    if (isNumber(token)) st.push(Integer.parseInt(token));
    else {
        int b = st.pop(), a = st.pop();
        st.push(apply(a, b, token));
    }
}
// Binary operand order matters for '-' and '/'.
```

**ST12 — Infix arithmetic with precedence and parentheses.** **Recognition:** Use when operators occur between numbers and must be evaluated in precedence and grouping order.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> values = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
java.util.ArrayDeque<Character> ops = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
// Tokenize multi-digit numbers; reduce higher/equal precedence operators first.
// '(' opens a new reduction boundary, ')' drains to that boundary.
```

**ST13 — Nested encoded-string frames.** **Recognition:** Use when opening delimiters save earlier output state and closers combine a nested fragment.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> repeats = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
java.util.ArrayDeque<StringBuilder> parents = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
StringBuilder current = new StringBuilder();
// On open: save current + repetition count; reset current.
// On close: combine nested current with restored parent.
```

**ST14 — Paired parentheses with direction-switch traversal.** **Recognition:** Use when nested substring reversals can be represented by switching scan direction at matching parentheses.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> openings = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
int[] mate = new int[s.length()];
for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
    if (s.charAt(i) == '(') openings.push(i);
    else if (s.charAt(i) == ')') { int j = openings.pop(); mate[i] = j; mate[j] = i; }
}
// Walk i with direction +1 initially. On '(' or ')', jump to mate[i]
// and reverse direction; otherwise append s.charAt(i). Each character visited O(1) times.
```

**ST15 — Nearest qualifying element with monotonic index stack.** **Recognition:** Use when each index wants the next larger/smaller value to its right, perhaps circularly or as a distance.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> unresolved = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
    while (!unresolved.isEmpty() && a[i] > a[unresolved.peek()]) {
        int j = unresolved.pop(); // i is next greater for j
    }
    unresolved.push(i);
}
// Adapt comparison, index->value lookup, circular pass, or output distance.
```

**ST16 — Monotonic stack with accumulated visibility.** **Recognition:** Use when elements block one another and each incoming height resolves previously unblocked ones.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> heights = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (int h : people) {
    int seen = 0;
    while (!heights.isEmpty() && heights.peek() < h) {
        heights.pop(); seen++;
    }
    if (!heights.isEmpty()) seen++; // blocking taller/equal person
    heights.push(h);
    // Adapt traversal direction and where seen is recorded.
}
```

**ST17 — Second-greater deferred resolution.** **Recognition:** Use when the second, not first, greater element is needed and candidates pass through two unresolved stages.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> first = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> second = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
    while (!second.isEmpty() && a[second.peek()] < a[i]) { /* resolve second */ }
    // Transfer indices whose FIRST greater is a[i] from first to second,
    // preserving their order (use a temporary buffer); then push i to first.
}
```

**ST18 — Online previous-greater span compression.** **Recognition:** Use when each incoming value absorbs a maximal suffix of previous values no larger than it.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<int[]> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
int span = 1;
while (!st.isEmpty() && st.peek()[0] <= price) span += st.pop()[1];
st.push(new int[]{price, span});
// The top pair compresses the current same-or-less suffix.
```

**ST19 — Previous/next smaller boundaries and histogram spans.** **Recognition:** Use when a contiguous block is limited by the nearest strictly lower boundary around a chosen height.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i <= height.length; i++) {
    int curr = i == height.length ? 0 : height[i];
    while (!st.isEmpty() && height[st.peek()] > curr) {
        int mid = st.pop();
        int left = st.isEmpty() ? -1 : st.peek();
        long width = i - left - 1L; // mid's maximal constrained width
    }
    if (i < height.length) st.push(i);
}
// Choose consistent tie policy for equal heights.
```

**ST20 — Monotonic boundary contribution accounting.** **Recognition:** Use when aggregating minima/maxima over ALL subarrays without enumerating them.

```java
int[] left = new int[n], right = new int[n];
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
// left[i] = distance to previous strictly smaller
// right[i] = distance to next smaller-or-equal (tie on ONE side)
// contribution of a[i] as minimum = a[i] * left[i] * right[i]
```

**ST21 — Lexicographically optimal removal stack.** **Recognition:** Use when deleting a bounded number of elements creates the lexicographically smallest feasible remaining sequence.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
int remainingDeletes = toRemove;
for (int x : a) {
    while (remainingDeletes > 0 && !st.isEmpty() && st.peek() > x) {
        st.pop(); remainingDeletes--;
    }
    st.push(x);
}
// Apply output length/uniqueness/leading-zero constraints per problem.
```

**ST22 — Collisions resolved by surviving stack top.** **Recognition:** Use when objects moving toward each other can collide, eliminating one or both, and each survivor can collide again.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> survivors = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (int x : a) {
    boolean alive = true;
    while (alive && x < 0 && !survivors.isEmpty() && survivors.peek() > 0) {
        int top = survivors.peek();
        if (top < -((long)x)) { survivors.pop(); continue; }
        if (top == -((long)x)) survivors.pop();
        alive = false;
    }
    if (alive) survivors.push(x);
}
```

**ST23 — 132-pattern range-candidate stack.** **Recognition:** Use when seeking indices i<j<k with low[i] < value[k] < value[j] without enumerating triples.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> possibleHigh = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
long bestMiddle = Long.MIN_VALUE;
for (int i = a.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    if (a[i] < bestMiddle) { /* pattern exists */ }
    while (!possibleHigh.isEmpty() && a[i] > possibleHigh.peek())
        bestMiddle = possibleHigh.pop();
    possibleHigh.push(a[i]);
}
```

**ST24 — Longest balanced-parentheses segment.** **Recognition:** Use when the objective is the longest CONTIGUOUS valid bracket substring, not global validity.

```java
java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
st.push(-1); // last invalid boundary
for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
    if (s.charAt(i) == '(') st.push(i);
    else {
        st.pop();
        if (st.isEmpty()) st.push(i);
        else { int length = i - st.peek(); /* maximize */ }
    }
}
```

## C13. T13 QUEUE, DEQUE & MONOTONIC QUEUE — COMPLETE ORDERED QUESTION PLAN (LOCKED)

**Scope:** **15 globally unique LeetCode questions in 12 algorithm families (Q1–Q12)**. **Progress:** 0/15. **Next:** LC **232**. Covers FIFO/LIFO emulation, bounded ring buffers, chronological eviction, index-turn simulations, balancing paired deques, monotonic maxima, monotonic DP, prefix-deque optimization, and feasibility queues. A `Deque` is a double-ended sequence, whereas a **monotonic deque** retains only undominated candidates; never confuse its order with a priority queue or the original FIFO queue. Java 21 `ArrayDeque` is preferred for ordinary in-memory queues; fixed-capacity design tasks require explicit storage.

### T13 LOCKED question registry

| Seq | LC | Family; recognition, key concept and canonical optimized Java 21 strategy | EXACT change from previous implementation within family |
|---:|---:|---|---|
| **Q1 — Queue implemented with two stacks: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 01 | **232** | Q1; FIFO operations when only LIFO primitives are available. Use input/output stacks and lazy transfer on demand; amortized O(1) per operation. | BASE: transfer input into output only when output is empty; newest pushes remain in input. |
| **Q2 — Stack implemented with queues: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 02 | **225** | Q2; LIFO operations when queue primitives only are permitted. Maintain one queue, rotating older elements after each push so newest is front; push O(n), pop/top O(1). | NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY: reordering one queue to expose latest element, not lazy two-stack transfer. |
| **Q3 — Fixed-capacity circular buffer: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 03 | **622** | Q3; Fixed-sized FIFO queue with wraparound and no shifting. Use int array, head index, size, and modulo capacity for tail. O(1) operations. | BASE: tail=(head+size)%capacity; head advances on removal; size separates empty from full. |
| 04 | **641** | Q3; Support both-end insertion/removal under fixed capacity. Reuse ring storage, head, size; front insert decrements head modulo n and rear removal reduces size. O(1) operations. | Retain circular-array head/size invariant; add pushFront/popRear, circular decrement, and peekRear. |

O(1) each. | NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY: paired deques with a size-balance invariant; not sequentially derived from senate turns. |

O(n). | Keep head expiry by index and dominated-tail removal; replace compared score nums[index] with computed dp[index], and emit max(dp[n-1]). |
| 11 | **1425** | Q8; Maximum nonempty constrained subsequence sum, consecutive selected positions ≤k apart. Same deque of descending DP values; allow restarting at current number when best previous contribution ≤0. O(n). | Retain DP max deque and k-gap eviction; change transition to nums[i]+max(0,bestPrev) and aggregate global maximum instead of final DP state. |
| **Q9 — Coordinate-range monotonic candidate deque: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(n). | NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY (cross-pattern reuse of Q8 deque mechanics): replace index-window expiry with coordinate distance and score with y-x; query before insertion. |
| **Q10 — Variable-window maximum plus additive budget: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

**Code-evolution integrity:** Q3 622→641 modifies one circular-buffer representation; Q8 239→1696→1425 retains the decreasing candidate deque but changes the value being maximized and DP objective. All other changes in the data-structure invariant have an explicit **NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** marker. `1499` reuses a learned deque primitive across families but has its own coordinate-expiry invariant. No claims of direct succession between unrelated families.

### T13 recognition clues and BASIC Java 21 templates

Each template is a **minimal invariant sketch, not a complete submitted solution**. Display once before that family's first practice question; use actual problem-specific operations in the full solution.

**Q1 — Queue from two stacks.** Clue: implement FIFO using only push/pop of stacks; transfer only when output stack is empty.

```java
Deque<Integer> in = new ArrayDeque<>(), out = new ArrayDeque<>();
// enqueue: in.push(x);
if (out.isEmpty()) while (!in.isEmpty()) out.push(in.pop());
// dequeue/front: out.pop() / out.peek(); guard nonempty.
```

**Q2 — Stack from queues.** Clue: implement LIFO using only FIFO operations; rotate all prior elements behind the new head.

```java
Queue<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.offer(x);
for (int i = 0, moves = q.size() - 1; i < moves; i++) q.offer(q.remove());
// q.peek() is newest element; q.remove() pops it.
```

**Q3 — Ring buffer.** Clue: fixed capacity, both ends accessible without shifting; wrap positions using modulo capacity.

```java
int[] buffer = new int[capacity];
int head = 0, size = 0;
// rear insertion: buffer[(head + size) % capacity] = x; size++;
// front insertion: head = (head - 1 + capacity) % capacity; buffer[head] = x; size++;
// front removal: head = (head + 1) % capacity; size--;
// rear removal: size--;
// Guard capacity>0, empty (size==0), full (size==capacity).
```

**Q4 — Chronological eviction.** Clue: input timestamps arrive in order and only recent events remain active.

```java
Deque<Integer> times = new ArrayDeque<>();
times.addLast(now);
while (!times.isEmpty() && times.peekFirst() < now - horizon) times.removeFirst();
// times.size() counts surviving events, inclusive lower bound.
```

**Q5 — Position queue.** Clue: values need to be arranged so a future reveal/rotate process produces a target order.

```java
Queue<Integer> slots = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) slots.offer(i);
for (int value : sortedValues) {
    result[slots.remove()] = value;
    if (!slots.isEmpty()) slots.offer(slots.remove());
}
```

**Q6 — Paired turn queues.** Clue: two teams/participants alternate in cyclic order; the earliest active index acts first.

```java
Queue<Integer> a = new ArrayDeque<>(), b = new ArrayDeque<>();
// enqueue initial indices by group.
while (!a.isEmpty() && !b.isEmpty()) {
    int ia = a.remove(), ib = b.remove();
    if (ia < ib) a.offer(ia + n);
    else b.offer(ib + n);
}
```

**Q7 — Balanced two-deque middle.** Clue: O(1) operations at front/middle/back; left half has same number as right or one extra.

```java
Deque<Integer> left = new ArrayDeque<>(), right = new ArrayDeque<>();
// After any mutation, restore left.size()==right.size() or left.size()==right.size()+1.
while (left.size() > right.size() + 1) right.addFirst(left.removeLast());
while (left.size() < right.size()) left.addLast(right.removeFirst());
// Middle is left.peekLast(); maintain problem's exact middle tie behavior.
```

**Q8 — Fixed-width decreasing candidate deque.** Clue: each index needs best eligible previous index in a fixed-width range, using original array values or computed DP values.

```java
Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i < score.length; i++) {
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && dq.peekFirst() < i - k) dq.removeFirst();
    // Query dq.peekFirst() for best eligible PREVIOUS index when doing DP.
    // Calculate score[i] from available candidate, then insert it:
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && score[dq.peekLast()] <= score[i]) dq.removeLast();
    dq.addLast(i);
}
// For window maxima, query AFTER insertion with the correct window bound.
```

**Q9 — Coordinate-range decreasing candidates.** Clue: each new increasing-x point needs maximum transformed key among previous points within distance k.

```java
Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (int j = 0; j < points.length; j++) {
    long x = points[j][0];
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && x - points[dq.peekFirst()][0] > k) dq.removeFirst();
    // If nonempty: evaluate current y+x plus previous best y-x BEFORE insertion.
    long key = (long) points[j][1] - points[j][0];
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && (long)points[dq.peekLast()][1] - points[dq.peekLast()][0] <= key) dq.removeLast();
    dq.addLast(j);
}
```

**Q10 — Variable-width deque plus sum.** Clue: a subarray's feasibility depends on its maximum and an additive running cost multiplied by its length.

```java
Deque<Integer> maxQ = new ArrayDeque<>();
long sum = 0;
int left = 0;
for (int right = 0; right < charge.length; right++) {
    sum += running[right];
    while (!maxQ.isEmpty() && charge[maxQ.peekLast()] <= charge[right]) maxQ.removeLast();
    maxQ.addLast(right);
    while (!maxQ.isEmpty() && charge[maxQ.peekFirst()] + (long)(right-left+1)*sum > budget) {
        if (maxQ.peekFirst() == left) maxQ.removeFirst();
        sum -= running[left++];
    }
    // [left,right] is valid; inspect its length.
}
```

**Q11 — Increasing prefix deque.** Clue: seek shortest qualifying subarray while negative elements invalidate ordinary sliding-window sum monotonicity.

```java
long[] prefix = new long[n + 1];
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + nums[i];
Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && prefix[i] - prefix[dq.peekFirst()] >= target) {
        int start = dq.removeFirst();
        // consider length i - start
    }
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && prefix[dq.peekLast()] >= prefix[i]) dq.removeLast();
    dq.addLast(i);
}
```

**Q12 — Sorted matching with two-end choice.** Clue: after sorting jobs and capacity, an eligible task may consume either an unboosted strong worker or a boosted weaker worker; selecting easiest/hardest needs deque ends.

```java
Deque<Integer> eligible = new ArrayDeque<>();
// In feasibility check for chosen task count t, process chosen workers in sorted order.
// Add tasks whose requirement <= worker + strength into eligible.
// If hardest eligible task <= worker: removeLast() with no pill.
// Else if pills available: removeFirst() and consume a pill.
// Else feasibility fails. Restore queue afresh per tested t.
```

| Josephus recurrence over circular elimination | **T27 Math & Number Theory**, LC 1823 candidate; not assigned here because O(n) recurrence beats queue simulation |
| Thread-safe bounded blocking queues, producer/consumer, fairness, capacity and `Deque` contracts | Java 21 companion concept notes, not additional LeetCode ownership |

## C14. T14 LINKED LISTS — COMPLETE ORDERED QUESTION PLAN (LOCKED)

**Scope:** 38 globally unique LeetCode questions in 27 algorithm families (L1–L27). **Progress:** 0/38; **next:** LC **1290**. Java 21-compatible reusable patterns. A singly linked list provides sequential access, not O(1) random access. Decide whether the problem asks for *node identity*, *node value*, stable order, or structural mutation. Use `==` for node-identity comparison. Each family starts independently, except where clearly marked cross-family primitive reuse. Difficulty generally increases **within** a family; cross-family order is driven by the simplest entry point and reusable pointer primitives.

### T14 recognition clues and BASIC Java 21 templates

These are **small invariant demonstrations**, not complete LeetCode submissions. The practice chat must introduce the correct template **once**, before the foundation question of each family. Assume the standard LeetCode `ListNode` definition unless a problem defines a different `Node` type. In snippets, names such as `shouldRemove`, `Node` and `DoublyList` denote problem-specific methods/types; they are intentionally not standalone compilable solutions.

**L1 — One-pass payload aggregation.** **When to apply:** A linked sequence carries digits or delimiters, and an answer can be accumulated without mutating links.

```java
int value = 0;
for (ListNode node = head; node != null; node = node.next) {
    value = value * base + node.val; // base supplied by constraints
}
```

**L2 — In-place contiguous segment reversal.** **When to apply:** A contiguous set of `next` edges must reverse while node identity and O(1) auxiliary space are preserved.

```java
ListNode prev = null, curr = head;
while (curr != null) {
    ListNode next = curr.next;
    curr.next = prev;
    prev = curr;
    curr = next;
}
// prev is the head of the reversed segment.
```

**L3 — Local group reversal.** **When to apply:** Swap actual nodes, not just values; the operation repeats on bounded consecutive groups.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head), before = dummy;
while (before.next != null) {
    // Identify a complete group; reverse its links; reconnect;
    // then advance before to the previous group's new tail.
    break; // group-specific work goes here
}
```

**L4 — Fast/slow midpoint + second-half reversal.** **When to apply:** A middle position or symmetric nodes are needed without array conversion.

```java
ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
    slow = slow.next;
    fast = fast.next.next;
}
// slow is middle; optionally reverse/compare second half.
```

**L5 — Floyd cycle detection and entry.** **When to apply:** Nodes may revisit a predecessor and only O(1) extra memory is allowed.

```java
ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
    slow = slow.next;
    fast = fast.next.next;
    if (slow == fast) { /* meeting inside cycle */ break; }
}
```

**L6 — Sorted-list merging as primitive.** **When to apply:** Two sorted runs can be consumed by selecting smaller front node and relinking, without copying payloads.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(), tail = dummy;
while (a != null && b != null) {
    if (a.val <= b.val) { tail.next = a; a = a.next; }
    else { tail.next = b; b = b.next; }
    tail = tail.next;
}
tail.next = a != null ? a : b;
```

**L7 — Predicate-driven removal and sorted-run deduplication.** **When to apply:** Nodes are removed or retained based on a local predicate, and a sentinel simplifies head deletion.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head), prev = dummy;
while (prev.next != null) {
    if (shouldRemove(prev.next)) prev.next = prev.next.next;
    else prev = prev.next;
}
// shouldRemove(...) represents a problem-specific predicate.
```

**L8 — Fixed-distance two-pointer deletion.** **When to apply:** Remove the kth node measured backward from the tail in one scan.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
ListNode fast = dummy, slow = dummy;
for (int i = 0; i < gap; i++) fast = fast.next;
while (fast.next != null) { fast = fast.next; slow = slow.next; }
// slow.next identifies the node to unlink.
```

**L9 — Stable multi-chain partition.** **When to apply:** Nodes must be rearranged into categories preserving original order inside each category.

```java
ListNode leftDummy = new ListNode(), rightDummy = new ListNode();
ListNode left = leftDummy, right = rightDummy;
for (ListNode node = head; node != null; ) {
    ListNode next = node.next; node.next = null;
    if (inFirstGroup(node)) { left.next = node; left = node; }
    else { right.next = node; right = node; }
    node = next;
}
left.next = rightDummy.next;
```

**L10 — Split–reverse–weave reordering.** **When to apply:** First and last nodes must alternate without allocating a new list.

```java
// 1. Find midpoint with slow/fast pointers.
// 2. Reverse the second half using L2 reversal.
// 3. Alternate nodes from first half and reversed second half.
// Save each half's next node before changing links.
```

**L11 — Circular tail connection and cut.** **When to apply:** Right rotation must move an entire suffix ahead of prefix while preserving relative order.

```java
int length = 1;
ListNode tail = head;
while (tail.next != null) { tail = tail.next; length++; }
int shift = k % length;
// If shift != 0: tail.next = head; cut after length - shift nodes.
```

**L12 — Least-significant-digit-first list arithmetic.** **When to apply:** Digits appear in reverse numerical order so carry propagates in the list traversal direction.

```java
int carry = 0;
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(), tail = dummy;
while (a != null || b != null || carry != 0) {
    int sum = carry;
    if (a != null) { sum += a.val; a = a.next; }
    if (b != null) { sum += b.val; b = b.next; }
    tail.next = new ListNode(sum % 10);
    tail = tail.next; carry = sum / 10;
}
```

**L13 — Most-significant-digit-first list arithmetic.** **When to apply:** Digits appear highest place first so carry must be computed from the end.

```java
java.util.Deque<Integer> digits = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (ListNode node = head; node != null; node = node.next) {
    digits.push(node.val);
}
// Pop from each input stack to add from low-order digits;
// prepend each resulting digit node.
```

**L14 — Identity-based list intersection.** **When to apply:** Two lists may share actual node objects and their prefix lengths may differ.

```java
ListNode a = headA, b = headB;
while (a != b) {
    a = a == null ? headB : a.next;
    b = b == null ? headA : b.next;
}
// Same reference (or both null).
```

**L15 — Copying non-linear random-pointer links.** **When to apply:** Besides `next`, each node has a `random` edge and clone must preserve graph identity.

```java
for (Node p = head; p != null; ) {
    Node copy = new Node(p.val);
    copy.next = p.next; p.next = copy;
    p = copy.next;
}
// Assign copy.random = original.random == null ? null : original.random.next;
// Then separate interleaved originals and copies.
```

**L16 — Flattening multilevel doubly linked nodes.** **When to apply:** Nested `child` chains must be spliced into an ordered doubly linked traversal.

```java
java.util.Deque<Node> deferred = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
Node curr = head;
while (curr != null) {
    if (curr.child != null) {
        if (curr.next != null) deferred.push(curr.next);
        // splice child with consistent prev/next; clear child
    } else if (curr.next == null && !deferred.isEmpty()) {
        // attach deferred next; update both pointers
    }
    curr = curr.next;
}
```

**L17 — Doubly linked LRU order + hash index.** **When to apply:** O(1) cache access must move a key to most-recent position and evict least-recent.

```java
java.util.Map<Integer, Node> nodes = new java.util.HashMap<>();
// Sentinel doubly-linked head/tail nodes.
// On read/write hit: detach node and append at MRU tail.
// On overflow: evict head.next (LRU), remove map entry.
```

**L18 — Frequency buckets with LRU tie-breaking.** **When to apply:** Evict lowest access frequency, breaking ties by least-recent use.

```java
java.util.Map<Integer, Node> byKey = new java.util.HashMap<>();
java.util.Map<Integer, DoublyList> byFrequency = new java.util.HashMap<>();
int minFrequency = 0;
// Touch: unlink from frequency f, increment to f+1 and append to f+1 list.
// Evict oldest from minFrequency list.
```

**L19 — Implementing indexed linked-list API.** **When to apply:** Users request head/tail/index-based insert/delete; pointer updates must handle index zero and out-of-range.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(); int size = 0;
// For indexed insert/delete, walk from dummy to predecessor.
// Reject invalid index; relink next exactly once; update size.
```

**L20 — Linked-list values with monotonic stack.** **When to apply:** Find next greater *to the right* for each list node without random access.

```java
java.util.List<Integer> values = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
java.util.List<Integer> answer = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
java.util.Deque<Integer> pending = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
for (ListNode p = head; p != null; p = p.next) {
    int i = values.size();
    while (!pending.isEmpty() && values.get(pending.peek()) < p.val)
        answer.set(pending.pop(), p.val);
    values.add(p.val); answer.add(0); pending.push(i);
}
```

**L21 — Prefix sums and node-boundary bypass.** **When to apply:** Remove maximal zero-sum contiguous chains without one-by-one trial deletion.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
java.util.Map<Long, ListNode> last = new java.util.HashMap<>();
long prefix = 0;
for (ListNode p = dummy; p != null; p = p.next) {
    prefix += p.val; last.put(prefix, p);
}
prefix = 0;
for (ListNode p = dummy; p != null; p = p.next) {
    prefix += p.val; p.next = last.get(prefix).next;
}
```

**L22 — Reservoir sampling on unknown-length list.** **When to apply:** Choose one linked-list node uniformly at random without knowing length in advance.

```java
int seen = 0, chosen = 0;
for (ListNode p = head; p != null; p = p.next) {
    if (java.util.concurrent.ThreadLocalRandom.current().nextInt(++seen) == 0)
        chosen = p.val;
}
```

**L23 — In-place deletion without head access.** **When to apply:** Only a non-tail node reference is provided, not a list head or preceding pointer.

```java
// Assumption: node != null and node.next != null.
node.val = node.next.val;
node.next = node.next.next;
```

**L24 — Cut-and-splice segment editing.** **When to apply:** Replace a contiguous interval with an existing list, preserving connected outer segments.

```java
// Locate predecessor before the cut and successor after it.
ListNode before = head, after = head;
// Move before to index a-1; after to index b+1.
// before.next = replacementHead; replacementTail.next = after.
```

**L25 — Suffix-dominance node filtering.** **When to apply:** Keep a node only if no greater node occurs to its right.

```java
// Reverse the list (L2 helper).
int maxSeen = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
ListNode prev = null, curr = reversedHead;
while (curr != null) {
    if (curr.val >= maxSeen) { maxSeen = curr.val; prev = curr; }
    else prev.next = curr.next;
    curr = curr.next;
}
// Reverse remaining list again.
```

**L26 — Local extrema with index-distance tracking.** **When to apply:** Find spacing between consecutive local minima/maxima in a singly linked list.

```java
int index = 1, first = -1, previous = -1;
for (ListNode a = head, b = head == null ? null : head.next;
     b != null && b.next != null; a = b, b = b.next) {
    index++;
    if ((b.val > a.val && b.val > b.next.val)
        || (b.val < a.val && b.val < b.next.val)) {
        if (first < 0) first = index;
        // Update shortest distance to previous and longest from first.
        previous = index;
    }
}
```

**L27 — Delimiter-bounded list aggregation.** **When to apply:** Values between marker nodes form independent groups which become output nodes.

```java
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(), tail = dummy;
int sum = 0;
for (ListNode p = head.next; p != null; p = p.next) {
    if (p.val == 0) {
        tail.next = new ListNode(sum); tail = tail.next; sum = 0;
    } else sum += p.val;
}
```

## C15. T15 RECURSION — COMPLETE ORDERED QUESTION PLAN (LOCKED)

**Scope:** 12 distinct LeetCode questions in 9 algorithm families (RC1–RC9). **Progress: 0/12; next: LC 50.** Recursion here is the primary *control structure or recursive grammar invariant*, not simply an alternative syntax for an existing linear, graph, DP, or linked-list solution. Each family has its own base case and strictly decreasing measure. Recognize when a problem can be reduced to a smaller instance without generating every possibility. Java 21 templates below are short teaching sketches, not full LeetCode submissions.

### T15 LOCKED question registry

| Seq | LC | Family; problem-recognition clue, core invariant, canonical optimized Java 21 strategy | EXACT modification from preceding solution **within the same family** |
|---:|---:|---|---|
| **RC1 — Exponentiation by squaring: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 01 | **50** | RC1; large integer exponent, repeated multiplication too costly. Recurse on `n/2`, square the result, multiply once for odd exponent; safely convert signed exponent to `long`, invert base for negative. O(log abs(n)) time/stack. | **BASE:** recursive smaller exponent, base case exponent zero, reuse one half-result rather than two recursive calls. |
| **RC2 — Implicit binary-sequence indexing: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 02 | **779** | RC2; requested symbol lies in exponentially expanding binary grammar; do not generate rows. Recurse to parent at position `(k+1)/2`, then flip for even-position child. O(n) recursion depth, O(1) work per level. | **BASE:** map a 1-indexed position to a smaller generation and reconstruct a bit from parent plus child parity. |
| 03 | **1545** | RC2; requested bit of recursively generated `left + 1 + reverse(invert(left))`. Compare `k` to midpoint `2^(n-1)`; left recurse unchanged, right map to reflected position and invert returned bit. | Replace direct parent position mapping with midpoint split; add middle base answer and right-half mirror-and-invert transformation. |
| **RC3 — Implicit concatenation with operation labels: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 04 | **3304** | RC3; target index in successively doubled text where appended half is shifted by one letter. Recurse/trace from smallest power-of-two block containing `k`: left unchanged, right map to first-half index and add one modulo 26. | **BASE:** halve an implicit doubled text, carry an additive character-offset across right-half choices; never materialize the text. |
| 05 | **3307** | RC3; each doubling step has a configurable shift/no-shift operation. Preserve index-halving recurrence and accumulate `operations[level]` only when traversing the appended half; use `long` indexing and modular letter shifts. | Replace hard-coded right-half `+1` with operation-specific `+operations[level]`, and navigate with 64-bit powers/positions. |
| **RC4 — Circular survivor index recurrence: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 06 | **1823** | RC4; repeated elimination of every kth item in a circle; survivor after n players depends only on survivor among n−1. Compute zero-based `winner(n)=(winner(n-1)+k)%n`, base `winner(1)=0`; convert to 1-indexed. O(n) time/stack. | **BASE:** solve smaller circle and translate survivor index when reintroducing the removed position. |
| **RC5 — Alternating elimination and transformed survivor coordinates: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 07 | **390** | RC5; remove alternating entries from opposite ends in repeated rounds. Recursive survivor transform on remaining `n/2`, with direction parity determining how the smaller solution maps back; O(log n) levels. | **BASE:** halve remaining candidate count, invert deletion direction, algebraically map the surviving index/value back; *not* a mere change of Josephus `k`. |
| **RC6 — Recursive descent of nested structural values: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 08 | **385** | RC6; bracket-delimited nested integers/lists form a recursive grammar. Maintain mutable cursor `pos`, parse signed integer or `[` followed by comma-separated child values and `]`; return a nested value; O(n) time, O(depth) call stack. | **BASE:** single cursor shared across nested `parseValue` calls; each call consumes exactly one grammar production and stops at its closing delimiter. |
| 09 | **726** | RC6; parenthesized chemical groups multiply nested atom counts. Replace list-builder return with map of element→count; parse atom names, multi-digit multipliers, and `(...)` recursively; on closing group multiply and merge counts, then lexicographically format. | Retain recursive cursor and delimiter-driven nested parse; change return from a nested list to composable frequency maps, and add group/atom multipliers plus ordered emission. |
| **RC7 — Recursive boolean-expression grammar: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 10 | **1106** | RC7; prefix boolean operators have parenthesized argument lists and recursive subexpressions. Parse a literal or operator followed by comma-separated nested operands; combine via `!`, `&`, `|`; O(n) time, O(depth) stack. | **BASE:** parser returns a boolean rather than a structural object; branch on operator token and fold child results with its identity/operator. |
| **RC8 — Recursive evaluation with lexical scopes: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 11 | **736** | RC8; nested arithmetic and `let` bind variable names with shadowing. Recursively parse/evaluate add/mult/let expressions, with scope snapshots or per-name stacks restored when returning; O(n²) worst-case with naive tokenization, implement cursor-based scanning to avoid repeated substrings. | **BASE:** recursive evaluation carries an environment; `let` creates scoped assignments and restores bindings on unwind. Distinct from boolean folding because evaluation depends on lexical binding and shadowing. |
| **RC9 — Recursive set-valued expression grammar: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 12 | **1096** | RC9; braces, union commas and implicit concatenation of alternatives create recursively nested sets of strings. Recursive-descent parse with `union` of terms and Cartesian-product concatenation of factors; deduplicate with sets and sort final strings. | **BASE:** grammar has two distinct composition operators (set union, Cartesian concatenation), plus nested brace expressions; exponential result size is inherent. Do not misclassify as enumerating a generic backtracking decision tree. |

**Sequence rationale:** RC1–RC5 teach decreasing numeric/positional state with independently justified invariants; RC6–RC9 progress into recursive grammar parsing, then evaluation with scoped state and set-valued operations. Medium/hard parser problems follow the core foundations. Separate families are deliberate: similar recursion syntax does not establish equivalent implementation logic.

### T15 recognition cues and BASIC Java 21 templates

**RC1 — Exponentiation by squaring.** Clue: associative repeated operation can divide exponent in half; each call returns a reusable subresult.

```java
static double powerPositive(double base, long exp) {
    if (exp == 0) return 1.0;
    double half = powerPositive(base, exp / 2);
    return (exp % 2 == 0) ? half * half : half * half * base;
}
// Normalize the sign of the caller's int exponent using long first.
```

**RC2 — Implicit recursive bit sequence.** Clue: want a single indexed bit from a recursively defined sequence; avoid constructing exponentially long strings.

```java
static int getBit(int level, long position) {
    if (level == 1) return 0;
    long midpoint = 1L << (level - 1); // only when level shift is within range
    if (position == midpoint) return 1;
    if (position < midpoint) return getBit(level - 1, position);
    long mirrored = 2 * midpoint - position;
    return getBit(level - 1, mirrored) ^ 1;
}
// Shows reflected/inverted half: adapt the recurrence to the chosen sequence.
```

**RC3 — Operation-labeled doubling.** Clue: the target lies in one half of an implicit duplicated block; right-half membership adds a transformation.

```java
static int offset(long k, int level, int[] operations) {
    if (level == 0) return 0;
    long half = 1L << (level - 1); // choose safe level bound
    if (k <= half) return offset(k, level - 1, operations);
    return (offset(k - half, level - 1, operations)
            + operations[level - 1]) % 26;
}
// For fixed-shift variation, replace operation lookup with 1.
```

**RC4 — Circular survivor recurrence.** Clue: after eliminating one person, the remainder is a rotated version of the same circle problem.

```java
static int winnerZeroBased(int n, int k) {
    if (n == 1) return 0;
    return (winnerZeroBased(n - 1, k) + k) % n;
}
```

**RC5 — Alternating elimination.** Clue: each round removes half the candidates and reverses the side from which elimination starts.

```java
static int survivor(int n, boolean fromLeft) {
    if (n == 1) return 1;
    int smaller = survivor(n / 2, !fromLeft);
    // Map 'smaller' back through the direction-dependent retained positions.
    return smaller; // invariant sketch, not the complete recurrence
}
```

**RC6 — Nested structural parsing.** Clue: `[`/`(`/matching closer encloses a recursively repeated grammar, and the parser must return at the matching close.

```java
class CursorParser {
    private final String text;
    private int pos;
    CursorParser(String text) { this.text = text; }
    void parseGroup() {
        pos++; // consume opening delimiter
        while (pos < text.length() && text.charAt(pos) != ')') {
            // parse atom or recursively parse nested group
        }
        pos++; // consume matching ')'
    }
}
// Return NestedInteger for one grammar; return a count map for another.
```

**RC7 — Boolean grammar evaluation.** Clue: each prefix operator applies to a parenthesized sequence of recursively evaluated boolean children.

```java
boolean parseBoolean() {
    char token = text.charAt(pos++);
    if (token == 't') return true;
    if (token == 'f') return false;
    pos++; // '(' after operator
    // Recursively parse arguments separated by commas, fold with token.
    // Consume ')' and return folded boolean.
    return false; // placeholder for computed result
}
```

**RC8 — Lexically scoped recursive evaluation.** Clue: a nested expression can reference variables bound by an enclosing expression; local assignment must not escape its scope.

```java
int evalExpression(Map<String,Integer> bindings) {
    // Read next expression (literal, variable, add, mult, or let).
    // For let: copy or push scoped bindings before evaluating its body.
    // On return, restore outer bindings (or discard scoped copy).
    return 0; // placeholder for grammar-specific evaluated value
}
```

**RC9 — Set-valued expression grammar.** Clue: nested unions and concatenations require different ways to combine recursive results.

```java
static Set<String> concat(Set<String> left, Set<String> right) {
    Set<String> result = new HashSet<>();
    for (String a : left)
        for (String b : right) result.add(a + b);
    return result;
}
// parseExpression: union terms; parseTerm: concatenate factors.
```

### T15 completeness and strict ownership boundaries

| Essential recursion concept | T15 coverage / explicit other owner |
|---|---|
| Base case, progress measure, stack frames, return-value composition, recursion tree, O(depth) auxiliary stack | T15 RC1–RC5 companion lessons and template introductions |
| Numeric argument reduction by halves / reuse one subresult | T15 RC1, LC 50 |
| Implicit exponential string/tree indexing without constructing nodes | T15 RC2–RC3, LC 779/1545/3304/3307 |
| Recursive state-index mapping of a smaller survivor problem | T15 RC4–RC5, LC 1823/390 |
| Recursive descent, cursor ownership, matched delimiters, group closure | T15 RC6–RC9, LC 385/726/1106/736/1096 |
| Propagating environment, lexical shadowing, restoration across recursive return | T15 RC8, LC 736 |
| Returning collections from recursion and composition of union vs Cartesian product | T15 RC9, LC 1096 |
| Tail recursion versus ordinary recursion, JVM lack of guaranteed tail-call optimization, stack-overflow risks, replacing deep recursion with explicit stack/loop | T15 Java 21 companion concept, not a fabricated LeetCode assignment |
| Recursive array / linked-list manipulation and pointer restoration | **T14 Linked Lists**, LC 206/92/25/143; **T01 Arrays** and **T03 Two Pointers** own primary scanning/reversal solutions |
| Recursive DFS/BFS and tree traversal, construction, LCA, height | **T20 Trees & BST**, not repeated here |
| Recursive graph DFS and visited-state traversal | **T22 Graphs**, not repeated here |
| Enumerative choice trees, undo/pruning, subsets, permutations, combinations | **T16 Backtracking** |
| Merge/divide/combine, inversion counts, recursive merge sort, geometric divide-and-conquer | **T17 Divide & Conquer** |
| Memoization, overlapping subproblems, Fibonacci/tribonacci, knapsack and state DP | **T25 Dynamic Programming** (e.g., LC 509/1137); no exponential recursive solution falsely presented as canonical optimized |
| Bitmask recursion / XOR / exponent modular math as primary invariant | **T18 Bit Manipulation** and **T27 Math & Number Theory**, respectively |
| Recursive arithmetic parsing already owned by stack topic | **T12 Stack & Monotonic Stack**, e.g., LC 224/227/394; distinct T15 parser IDs do not overlap |
| Dictionary trie search and recursive prefix DFS | **T24 Trie & Prefix Trees** |

**Quality note:** Some recursive routines can be implemented iteratively and some parser problems can be solved with explicit stacks. The plan selects recursion where it directly exposes the problem's inductive structure. `long` is used for exponent sign/index growth; recursive depth is explicit. The Java templates are *invariants*, not the question solutions. In particular RC5/RC7/RC8 leave grammar-dependent return logic as comments instead of claiming to solve a problem.
