### T20 family recognition clues and BASIC Java 21 templates

The following are **invariant sketches**, not complete LeetCode submissions. Assume LeetCode-provided `TreeNode`/`Node` classes, appropriate `java.util` imports, and per-call fields where needed. Each template is shown once in the practice chat at the start of its family. When one family borrows a previously covered primitive (e.g., inorder or BFS), that is cross-family reuse, not a fabricated sequential link.

**T1 — Bottom-up height and subtree metrics.** **When to apply:** A node result depends on recursively computed child heights; O(n) postorder visits.

```java
int height(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 0;
    int l = height(node.left), r = height(node.right);
    // combine child heights; optionally update a global best/flag
    return 1 + Math.max(l, r);
}
```

**T2 — Two-tree structural comparison and mirror symmetry.** **When to apply:** Two root references are compared recursively, including cross-child symmetry.

```java
boolean match(TreeNode a, TreeNode b) {
    if (a == null || b == null) return a == b;
    return a.val == b.val && match(a.left, b.left)
            && match(a.right, b.right);
}
```

**T3 — In-place tree rewiring.** **When to apply:** Modify child pointers without creating a replacement tree.

```java
void mutate(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return;
    // Choose traversal order required by the rewrite.
    mutate(node.right);
    mutate(node.left);
    // Rewire node.left / node.right; for flattening, keep previous node.
}
```

**T4 — Breadth-first per-level aggregation.** **When to apply:** A question depends on level order, per-depth statistics, or level-based transformations.

```java
Deque<TreeNode> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.add(root); // guard root != null
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int size = q.size();
    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        TreeNode node = q.removeFirst();
        // collect or inspect this level
        if (node.left != null) q.addLast(node.left);
        if (node.right != null) q.addLast(node.right);
    }
}
```

**T5 — Next-right link construction.** **When to apply:** Populate next pointers among peers, and avoid linking across level boundaries.

```java
Node level = root;
while (level != null) {
    Node dummy = new Node(0), tail = dummy;
    for (Node cur = level; cur != null; cur = cur.next) {
        if (cur.left != null) { tail.next = cur.left; tail = tail.next; }
        if (cur.right != null) { tail.next = cur.right; tail = tail.next; }
    }
    level = dummy.next;
}
```

**T6 — Indexed breadth-first width.** **When to apply:** Positions in a conceptual complete tree define span width even across null gaps.

```java
record Pos(TreeNode node, long index) {}
Deque<Pos> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.add(new Pos(root, 0));
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int size = q.size(); long base = q.peekFirst().index();
    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        Pos p = q.removeFirst();
        long normalized = p.index() - base;
        // enqueue children at 2*normalized and 2*normalized+1
    }
}
```

**T7 — Root-to-leaf path state.** **When to apply:** Carry additive/ordered state along a root-to-leaf path; only leaves complete candidates.

```java
void dfs(TreeNode node, long state) {
    if (node == null) return;
    long next = state + node.val; // replace path transition
    if (node.left == null && node.right == null) {
        // inspect completed root-to-leaf state
        return;
    }
    dfs(node.left, next); dfs(node.right, next);
}
```

**T8 — Prefix-sum frequency along DFS ancestry.** **When to apply:** Count downward paths starting anywhere, not just the root; ancestral prefixes determine answers.

```java
Map<Long,Integer> count = new HashMap<>();
count.put(0L, 1);
void dfs(TreeNode node, long prefix) {
    if (node == null) return;
    prefix += node.val;
    // add count[prefix-target] before incrementing this prefix
    count.merge(prefix, 1, Integer::sum);
    dfs(node.left, prefix); dfs(node.right, prefix);
    count.merge(prefix, -1, Integer::sum); // undo per branch
}
```

**T9 — String-valued root-to-leaf paths.** **When to apply:** Need a path string, not a numeric aggregate; append on entry and undo at return.

```java
void dfs(TreeNode node, StringBuilder path) {
    if (node == null) return;
    int saved = path.length();
    path.append(node.val);
    if (node.left == null && node.right == null) { /* record leaf */ }
    else { dfs(node.left, path); dfs(node.right, path); }
    path.setLength(saved);
}
```

**T10 — Ancestor extrema along DFS paths.** **When to apply:** Evaluate each descendant relative to previous ancestors’ min/max rather than all ancestor pairs.

```java
void dfs(TreeNode node, int minimum, int maximum) {
    if (node == null) return;
    minimum = Math.min(minimum, node.val);
    maximum = Math.max(maximum, node.val);
    // check path-dependent invariant
    dfs(node.left, minimum, maximum);
    dfs(node.right, minimum, maximum);
}
```

**T11 — General binary-tree lowest common ancestor.** **When to apply:** A subtree can report a matching target or propagated LCA; left and right signals merge.

```java
TreeNode lca(TreeNode node, TreeNode a, TreeNode b) {
    if (node == null || node == a || node == b) return node;
    TreeNode l = lca(node.left, a, b), r = lca(node.right, a, b);
    return l != null && r != null ? node : l != null ? l : r;
}
```

**T12 — Deepest-leaf postorder LCA.** **When to apply:** Find deepest descendant height and common root of all deepest leaves in one return object.

```java
record State(TreeNode root, int depth) {}
State solve(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return new State(null, 0);
    State l = solve(node.left), r = solve(node.right);
    if (l.depth() == r.depth()) return new State(node, l.depth() + 1);
    return l.depth() > r.depth() ? new State(l.root(), l.depth()+1)
                                  : new State(r.root(), r.depth()+1);
}
```

**T13 — BST ordered lookup and mutation.** **When to apply:** BST ordering eliminates entire subtrees during search or insert/delete.

```java
TreeNode find(TreeNode node, int key) {
    while (node != null && node.val != key)
        node = key < node.val ? node.left : node.right;
    return node;
}
```

**T14 — BST in-order sorted stream.** **When to apply:** Inorder traversal of BST produces ascending values, enabling adjacent/rank/run statistics.

```java
TreeNode current = root;
Deque<TreeNode> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
while (current != null || !stack.isEmpty()) {
    while (current != null) { stack.push(current); current = current.left; }
    current = stack.pop();
    // consume next sorted value
    current = current.right;
}
```

**T15 — Balanced/reconstructed binary trees from traversals.** **When to apply:** Build subtree root recursively from a bounded interval and a known root-selection rule.

```java
TreeNode build(int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo > hi) return null;
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2; // problem-specific root index
    TreeNode root = new TreeNode(values[mid]);
    root.left = build(lo, mid - 1);
    root.right = build(mid + 1, hi);
    return root;
}
```

**T16 — Tree construction from traversal partitions.** **When to apply:** Pre/postorder identifies root, inorder or subtree index isolates left and right ranges.

```java
Map<Integer,Integer> inPos = new HashMap<>();
// Build lookup from inorder value to position (values distinct).
TreeNode build(int inL, int inR) {
    if (inL > inR) return null;
    int value = preorder[preIndex++];
    int cut = inPos.get(value);
    TreeNode node = new TreeNode(value);
    node.left = build(inL, cut-1);
    node.right = build(cut+1, inR);
    return node;
}
```

**T17 — BST preorder with value bounds.** **When to apply:** Ordering permits direct construction without inorder array.

```java
int index = 0;
TreeNode build(long low, long high) {
    if (index == preorder.length) return null;
    int x = preorder[index];
    if (x <= low || x >= high) return null;
    index++;
    TreeNode n = new TreeNode(x);
    n.left = build(low, x); n.right = build(x, high);
    return n;
}
```

**T18 — Tree serialization and reconstruction.** **When to apply:** Encode both structure and values, then decode with matching recursive order.

```java
void encode(TreeNode n, StringBuilder out) {
    if (n == null) { out.append("#,"); return; }
    out.append(n.val).append(',');
    encode(n.left, out); encode(n.right, out);
}
// Decode with a shared preorder token index, including null markers.
```

**T19 — Recover BST by inorder inversions.** **When to apply:** Exactly two swapped values break ascending inorder order at one or two inversions.

```java
TreeNode previous = null, first = null, second = null;
void inorder(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return;
    inorder(node.left);
    if (previous != null && previous.val > node.val) {
        if (first == null) first = previous;
        second = node;
    }
    previous = node;
    inorder(node.right);
}
```

**T20 — Tree DP with include/exclude states.** **When to apply:** Optimal decision at node depends on whether direct parent/child is selected.

```java
long[] dp(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return new long[]{0, 0};
    long[] l = dp(node.left), r = dp(node.right);
    long take = node.val + l[0] + r[0];
    long skip = Math.max(l[0], l[1]) + Math.max(r[0], r[1]);
    return new long[]{skip, take};
}
```

**T21 — Camera-state postorder coverage.** **When to apply:** Local coverage status dictates whether a parent must place a camera.

```java
// 0 = uncovered, 1 = camera, 2 = covered
int cover(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 2;
    int l = cover(node.left), r = cover(node.right);
    if (l == 0 || r == 0) { cameras++; return 1; }
    if (l == 1 || r == 1) return 2;
    return 0;
}
```

**T22 — Postorder deletion and forest pruning.** **When to apply:** Must decide child links after processing descendants, then return null when deleting this node.

```java
TreeNode prune(TreeNode n) {
    if (n == null) return null;
    n.left = prune(n.left);
    n.right = prune(n.right);
    if (shouldRemove(n)) return null;
    return n;
}
```

**T23 — Subtree matching and repeated signatures.** **When to apply:** Recognize subtree equality via recursive structure/value signatures, then reuse a signature for every subtree.

```java
String signature(TreeNode n, Map<String,Integer> freq) {
    if (n == null) return "#";
    String left = signature(n.left, freq);
    String right = signature(n.right, freq);
    String key = "(" + left + "," + n.val + "," + right + ")";
    freq.merge(key, 1, Integer::sum);
    return key;
}
```

**T24 — Lazy BST inorder iterator.** **When to apply:** Support next() and hasNext() without flattening entire tree, using a left-spine stack.

```java
Deque<TreeNode> pending = new ArrayDeque<>();
void pushLeft(TreeNode n) {
    while (n != null) { pending.push(n); n = n.left; }
}
// next: pop one, pushLeft(its right).
```

**T25 — Coordinate ordering of tree nodes.** **When to apply:** Rows/columns come from BFS/DFS coordinates; output order imposes multiple sorted keys.

```java
record Cell(int col, int row, int value) {}
void visit(TreeNode n, int row, int col, List<Cell> cells) {
    if (n == null) return;
    cells.add(new Cell(col, row, n.val));
    visit(n.left, row+1, col-1, cells);
    visit(n.right, row+1, col+1, cells);
}
// Sort by col, then row, then value.
```

**T26 — Layer parity and node-order constraints.** **When to apply:** Validate constraints applying to levels and left-to-right node order.

```java
int depth = 0;
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int size = q.size(), prev = depth % 2 == 0 ? Integer.MIN_VALUE : Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int i=0; i<size; i++) {
        TreeNode n = q.removeFirst();
        // check parity and monotone relation against prev
        prev = n.val;
        // enqueue children
    }
    depth++;
}
```

**T27 — Complete tree positional structure.** **When to apply:** Validate no child can appear after a gap in breadth-first complete-tree order.

```java
Deque<TreeNode> q = new LinkedList<>(); // allows null entries
q.add(root); boolean gap = false;
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    TreeNode n = q.removeFirst();
    if (n == null) { gap = true; continue; }
    if (gap) { /* not complete */ }
    q.addLast(n.left); q.addLast(n.right);
}
```

**T28 — Tree as undirected neighborhood graph.** **When to apply:** Allow upward movement via parent references; BFS from arbitrary starting node.

```java
Map<TreeNode,TreeNode> parent = new HashMap<>();
// Populate parent map using one DFS/BFS from root.
Deque<TreeNode> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
Set<TreeNode> visited = new HashSet<>();
q.add(start); visited.add(start);
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    TreeNode node = q.removeFirst();
    // enqueue unvisited node.left, node.right, parent.get(node)
}
```

**T29 — Two-node route from common ancestor.** **When to apply:** Find root-to-node directions, discard common prefix and encode upward moves.

```java
boolean path(TreeNode n, int target, StringBuilder dirs) {
    if (n == null) return false;
    if (n.val == target) return true;
    dirs.append('L');
    if (path(n.left, target, dirs)) return true;
    dirs.setLength(dirs.length()-1);
    dirs.append('R');
    if (path(n.right, target, dirs)) return true;
    dirs.setLength(dirs.length()-1);
    return false;
}
```

**T30 — Euler-tour subtree exclusion queries.** **When to apply:** Many subtree-removal height queries require reusable DFS entry/exit intervals and prefix/suffix maxima.

```java
int time = 0;
void dfs(TreeNode n, int depth) {
    if (n == null) return;
    int entry = time++;
    depthAt[entry] = depth;
    dfs(n.left, depth+1); dfs(n.right, depth+1);
    // subtree node positions occupy [entry, time-1]
    exitByValue[n.val] = time-1;
}
```

**T31 — Rerooting DP on tree edges.** **When to apply:** Need answer for every possible root, so calculate one root then transfer across each edge.

```java
void sizes(TreeNode n) {
    if (n == null) return;
    size[n.val] = 1;
    // process children, accumulate sizes and distance sum for root
}
void reroot(TreeNode parent, TreeNode child, int n) {
    answer[child.val] = answer[parent.val] + n - 2 * size[child.val];
}
```

### T21 recognition clues and BASIC Java 21 templates

Each code example is an invariant sketch, not the complete solution. Identifiers such as `Item`, `Worker`, `Task`, comparator names and `transform` are problem-specific placeholders. In Java 21 prefer `PriorityQueue`, `Comparator.comparing...` or explicit `Integer.compare`/`Long.compare` over subtraction comparators; `peek()` does not remove and `poll()` does.

**PQ1 — Frequency-ranked Top K.** **Recognition:** Repeated values; need the k most frequent keys, optionally with lexical tie-breaks. Count in a HashMap; retain best candidates in a size-k min-heap.

```java
Map<String,Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
// Count each key, then maintain a heap whose head is the WORST retained candidate.
PriorityQueue<String> top = new PriorityQueue<>(worstFirstComparator);
for (String key : freq.keySet()) {
    top.offer(key);
    if (top.size() > k) top.poll();
}
```

**PQ2 — Online Kth-largest threshold.** **Recognition:** An unbounded stream asks for the kth largest after each insertion; store precisely the k largest seen so far.

```java
PriorityQueue<Integer> topK = new PriorityQueue<>();
void accept(int value, int k) {
    topK.offer(value);
    if (topK.size() > k) topK.poll();
}
// Once k values exist, topK.peek() is kth largest.
```

**PQ3 — Merge multiple ordered streams.** **Recognition:** K input lists are individually sorted; the smallest current head across lists is the next global element.

```java
record Entry(int value, int stream, int index) {}
PriorityQueue<Entry> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(Entry::value));
// Offer the first item of each nonempty stream.
while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    Entry e = pq.poll();
    // consume e; offer the NEXT item from e.stream only.
}
```

**PQ4 — Best-first enumeration of sorted pair candidates.** **Recognition:** Pair costs are monotone along sorted input dimensions; enumerate the next smallest frontier without materializing all pairs.

```java
record PairState(int i, int j) {}
PriorityQueue<PairState> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(pairCostComparator);
// Seed monotone frontier (one state per row/stream).
while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    PairState cur = pq.poll();
    // record candidate, then push only its next neighbor in the same stream.
}
```

**PQ5 — Two-heaps order statistic and lazy eviction.** **Recognition:** Need online median/percentile while values arrive, or a moving window with outgoing items.

```java
PriorityQueue<Integer> low = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
PriorityQueue<Integer> high = new PriorityQueue<>();
// All low <= all high; low.size() == high.size() OR low.size()==high.size()+1.
// Insert into correct side, rebalance; median from heap tops.
```

**PQ6 — Repeated largest-first transformations.** **Recognition:** At each step choose the current largest object, transform it, and reinsert the updated value.

```java
PriorityQueue<Long> max = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
for (long x : values) max.offer(x);
for (int step = 0; step < operations; step++) {
    long x = max.poll();
    long updated = transform(x); // problem-specific transformation
    max.offer(updated);
}
```

**PQ7 — Repeated smallest-first combination.** **Recognition:** Operations must combine the two globally smallest values; a min-heap preserves the next feasible merge.

```java
PriorityQueue<Long> min = new PriorityQueue<>();
for (long x : a) min.offer(x);
while (min.size() >= 2 && min.peek() < threshold) {
    long x = min.poll(), y = min.poll();
    min.offer(combine(x, y));
}
```

**PQ8 — Interval sweep with active deadline heap.** **Recognition:** Intervals become eligible at a sorted start/query coordinate, while a heap selects the active interval with the most useful end or length.

```java
Arrays.sort(intervals, Comparator.comparingInt(x -> x[0]));
PriorityQueue<Integer> active = new PriorityQueue<>();
int i = 0;
for (int point : sortedPoints) {
    while (i < intervals.length && intervals[i][0] <= point) active.offer(intervals[i++][1]);
    while (!active.isEmpty() && active.peek() < point) active.poll();
    // choose an active candidate; heap key may be end or length.
}
```

**PQ9 — Greedy allocate scarce resources to largest demands.** **Recognition:** A new resource unit can replace previously allocated small demands; keep the least valuable recipients in a min-heap.

```java
PriorityQueue<Integer> ladderUses = new PriorityQueue<>();
long consumed = 0;
for (int demand : nonnegativeDemands) {
    ladderUses.offer(demand);
    if (ladderUses.size() > freeResourceCount) consumed += ladderUses.poll();
    // stop when consumed exceeds paid-resource budget
}
```

**PQ10 — Retroactive resource replenishment.** **Recognition:** Can choose among previously passed refill opportunities; when resources fall short, take the largest available refill.

```java
PriorityQueue<Integer> available = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
long fuel = initialFuel;
for (int[] stop : orderedStopsAndDestination) {
    // spend distance since previous position
    while (fuel < requiredDistance && !available.isEmpty()) fuel += available.poll();
    // fail if still insufficient; otherwise offer this stop's refill quantity
}
```

**PQ11 — Schedule by deadline with reversible choices.** **Recognition:** After sorting by deadline, if cumulative duration becomes too large, discard the most time-consuming selected task.

```java
Arrays.sort(tasks, Comparator.comparingInt(x -> x[1]));
PriorityQueue<Integer> chosen = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
long time = 0;
for (int[] task : tasks) {
    time += task[0]; chosen.offer(task[0]);
    if (time > task[1]) time -= chosen.poll();
}
```

**PQ12 — Two-tier capital feasibility and reward choice.** **Recognition:** Sort candidates by unlocking requirement; move affordable candidates into a max-heap prioritized by reward.

```java
Arrays.sort(projects, Comparator.comparingLong(x -> x.capitalNeeded));
PriorityQueue<Integer> profits = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
for (int step = 0; step < k; step++) {
    while (next < projects.length && projects[next].capitalNeeded <= capital)
        profits.offer(projects[next++].profit);
    if (profits.isEmpty()) break;
    capital += profits.poll();
}
```

**PQ13 — Two-sided candidate hiring.** **Recognition:** Only first c and last c remaining candidates can be chosen; heaps track the eligible frontier with index ties.

```java
PriorityQueue<int[]> left = new PriorityQueue<>(costThenIndex);
PriorityQueue<int[]> right = new PriorityQueue<>(costThenIndex);
// Seed nonoverlapping first/last candidate prefixes.
// Each hire polls cheaper eligible head then replenishes from that side.
```

**PQ14 — Sort by bottleneck and retain best k companions.** **Recognition:** A chosen group score uses one shared minimum/maximum key and the sum of the other field. Sort by bottleneck; heap maintains best companion values.

```java
Arrays.sort(items, (a,b) -> Integer.compare(b.bottleneck, a.bottleneck));
PriorityQueue<Integer> chosen = new PriorityQueue<>(); // discard weakest companion
long sum = 0;
for (Item x : items) {
    chosen.offer(x.companion); sum += x.companion;
    if (chosen.size() > k) sum -= chosen.poll();
    // when size==k, score using current bottleneck.
}
```

**PQ15 — Bounded group with maximum companion as cost.** **Recognition:** Sort by unit price/ratio; a max-heap discards the most expensive selected companion to minimize sum within size-k group.

```java
Arrays.sort(workers, Comparator.comparingDouble(w -> w.ratio));
PriorityQueue<Integer> qualities = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
long qualitySum = 0;
for (Worker w : workers) {
    qualities.offer(w.quality); qualitySum += w.quality;
    if (qualities.size() > k) qualitySum -= qualities.poll();
    if (qualities.size() == k) { /* qualitySum * w.ratio */ }
}
```

**PQ16 — Maximum-frequency arrangement with adjacency constraints.** **Recognition:** Need reorder items so equal values are never adjacent. Select the highest remaining-frequency eligible values first.

```java
PriorityQueue<Node> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a,b) -> Integer.compare(b.count,a.count));
Node held = null;
while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    Node now = pq.poll();
    // emit now; restore previously held element AFTER choosing now
    if (held != null && held.count > 0) pq.offer(held);
    held = --now.count > 0 ? now : null;
}
```

**PQ17 — Cooldown scheduling with task frequencies.** **Recognition:** Each scheduled task is temporarily ineligible for n following slots; track ready max-frequency tasks and release times.

```java
PriorityQueue<Task> ready = new PriorityQueue<>(remainingCountDescending);
ArrayDeque<Release> cooling = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (long time = 0; jobsRemain; time++) {
    while (!cooling.isEmpty() && cooling.peekFirst().readyAt <= time) ready.offer(cooling.pollFirst().task);
    // if ready nonempty, run one job and delay its next eligibility
}
```

**PQ18 — Two-heap resource availability scheduler.** **Recognition:** Resources can be free or busy until a future time; use one min-heap for preferred free resource and one by release time.

```java
PriorityQueue<Integer> free = new PriorityQueue<>();
PriorityQueue<long[]> busy = new PriorityQueue<>((x,y) ->
    x[0] != y[0] ? Long.compare(x[0],y[0]) : Long.compare(x[1],y[1]));
// Before each arrival, release resources with releaseTime <= arrival.
// Prefer least-index free resource; otherwise delay until earliest release.
```

**PQ19 — Single-machine shortest-job-first simulation.** **Recognition:** Jobs arrive over time; at each idle/complete moment, choose available task by processing duration and original index.

```java
Arrays.sort(tasks, Comparator.comparingLong(x -> x.arrival));
PriorityQueue<Job> ready = new PriorityQueue<>(durationThenIndex);
long time = 0;
while (jobsRemain) {
    if (ready.isEmpty()) time = Math.max(time, nextArrival);
    while (nextArrival <= time) ready.offer(nextJob);
    Job j = ready.poll(); time += j.duration;
}
```

**PQ20 — Normalize and shrink extremes.** **Recognition:** Allowed multiplicative changes can be normalized first so only monotone reductions remain; max-heap repeatedly reduces current maximum.

```java
PriorityQueue<Integer> max = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
int minSeen = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
for (int x : nums) {
    int normalized = (x & 1) == 1 ? x * 2 : x; // validate overflow constraints
    max.offer(normalized); minSeen = Math.min(minSeen, normalized);
}
while (true) {
    int largest = max.poll();
    // update range (largest-minSeen), halve even largest, or stop when odd
}
```

**PQ21 — Heap-ordered best-first boundary flood.** **Recognition:** Surface levels are determined by the lowest currently reachable boundary; process cells in min-level order and elevate trapped neighbors.

```java
record Cell(int level, int row, int col) {}
PriorityQueue<Cell> boundary = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(Cell::level));
boolean[][] seen = new boolean[rows][cols];
// Seed all border cells once.
while (!boundary.isEmpty()) {
    Cell c = boundary.poll();
    for (int[] d : DIRECTIONS) { // each unseen neighbor
        // trapped += max(0, c.level - height[nr][nc]);
        // offer neighbor with level=max(c.level,height[nr][nc])
    }
}
```

**PQ22 — Best-first merged social feeds.** **Recognition:** Each followed user message history is sorted newest first; return top few across streams by pushing one head per stream.

```java
record FeedItem(long timestamp, Tweet node) {}
PriorityQueue<FeedItem> recent = new PriorityQueue<>((a,b) -> Long.compare(b.timestamp,a.timestamp));
// Seed each followed user's latest item.
while (!recent.isEmpty() && output.size() < limit) {
    FeedItem top = recent.poll(); output.add(top.node.id);
    if (top.node.next != null) recent.offer(new FeedItem(top.node.next.time,top.node.next));
}
```

### T22 recognition clues and BASIC Java 21 templates

**GR1 — Undirected graph DFS and reachability.** **When to apply:** Edges are bidirectional; explore each vertex once with a visited set.

```java
List<Integer>[] adj = new List[n];
for (int i=0;i<n;i++) adj[i]=new ArrayList<>();
for (int[] e: edges) { adj[e[0]].add(e[1]); adj[e[1]].add(e[0]); }
boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
ArrayDeque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
stack.push(source); seen[source]=true;
while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
    int u=stack.pop();
    for (int v:adj[u]) if (!seen[v]) {seen[v]=true; stack.push(v);}
}
```

**GR2 — Enumeration of all directed paths.** **When to apply:** Output every complete path, not merely visited vertices; maintain path-local mutable state.

```java
void dfs(int u, int target, int[][] graph, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> out) {
    if (u == target) {out.add(new ArrayList<>(path)); return;}
    for (int v:graph[u]) {
        path.add(v); dfs(v,target,graph,path,out); path.remove(path.size()-1);
    }
} // Only for an acyclic graph, or add path-visited cycle guards.
```

**GR3 — Bipartite graph two-coloring.** **When to apply:** Adjacent nodes must receive opposite colors; detect an odd-cycle contradiction.

```java
int[] color = new int[n]; // 0 uncolored, +1/-1 colors
for (int start=0;start<n;start++) if(color[start]==0) {
    ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>(); q.add(start); color[start]=1;
    while(!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u=q.poll();
        for(int v:adj[u]) {
            if(color[v]==0) {color[v]=-color[u];q.add(v);}
            else if(color[v]==color[u]) { /* conflict */ }
        }
    }
}
```

**GR4 — Course ordering with Kahn topological sorting.** **When to apply:** Directed dependencies require predecessor-before-successor order; remove zero-indegree vertices.

```java
int[] indegree = new int[n];
for (int u=0;u<n;u++) for(int v:adj[u]) indegree[v]++;
ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
for(int u=0;u<n;u++) if(indegree[u]==0) q.add(u);
int processed=0;
while(!q.isEmpty()) {
    int u=q.poll(); processed++;
    for(int v:adj[u]) if(--indegree[v]==0) q.add(v);
} // processed == n iff acyclic.
```

**GR5 — Reverse-topological elimination of unsafe vertices.** **When to apply:** Directed graph vertices are safe when all outgoing paths end; remove terminal vertices backward.

```java
int[] outdegree = new int[n];
List<Integer>[] reverse = new List[n];
for(int i=0;i<n;i++) reverse[i]=new ArrayList<>();
for(int u=0;u<n;u++) for(int v:graph[u]) {outdegree[u]++;reverse[v].add(u);}
ArrayDeque<Integer> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
for(int u=0;u<n;u++) if(outdegree[u]==0) q.add(u);
while(!q.isEmpty()) {int u=q.poll(); for(int p:reverse[u]) if(--outdegree[p]==0) q.add(p);}
```

**GR6 — Directed cycle in a functional graph.** **When to apply:** Every node has at most one outgoing edge; walk successors with current-walk timestamps.

```java
int[] seenWalk=new int[n], depth=new int[n];
for(int start=0;start<n;start++) if(seenWalk[start]==0) {
    int u=start, step=0, mark=start+1;
    while(u!=-1 && seenWalk[u]==0) {seenWalk[u]=mark;depth[u]=step++;u=next[u];}
    if(u!=-1 && seenWalk[u]==mark) {int cycleLen=step-depth[u]; /* cycle */}
}
```

**GR7 — Unweighted shortest paths by BFS.** **When to apply:** Every graph edge has unit cost; first BFS discovery gives minimum edge count.

```java
int[] dist=new int[n]; Arrays.fill(dist,-1);
ArrayDeque<Integer> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
dist[source]=0;q.add(source);
while(!q.isEmpty()) {
    int u=q.poll();
    for(int v:adj[u]) if(dist[v]==-1) {dist[v]=dist[u]+1;q.add(v);}
}
```

**GR8 — Shortest path on explicitly modeled state-transition graph.** **When to apply:** Physical movements/transfers encode transitions; BFS counts transitions, not physical distance.

```java
record State(int id, int steps) {}
ArrayDeque<State> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
Set<Integer> seen=new HashSet<>();
q.add(new State(start,0)); seen.add(start);
while(!q.isEmpty()) {
    State cur=q.poll();
    for(int next:transitions(cur.id())) if(seen.add(next)) q.add(new State(next,cur.steps()+1));
}
```

    for(int size=q.size();size>0;size--) {
        int i=q.poll();
        // enumerate valid index jumps, add only unseen indices
    }

**GR10 — Alternating-edge-label BFS.** **When to apply:** A valid path depends on last edge color; visit (vertex,nextColor) states rather than vertices.

```java
int[][] dist=new int[n][2]; for(int[] row:dist) Arrays.fill(row,-1);
ArrayDeque<int[]> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
for(int c=0;c<2;c++){dist[0][c]=0;q.add(new int[]{0,c});}
while(!q.isEmpty()) {
    int[] s=q.poll(); int u=s[0], color=s[1];
    for(int v:adjByColor[color][u]) if(dist[v][1-color]<0) {
        dist[v][1-color]=dist[u][color]+1;q.add(new int[]{v,1-color});
    }
}
```

**GR11 — BFS with extra resource state.** **When to apply:** Same position with different remaining budget/keys can have different future options; visited is state-aware.

```java
record State(int r,int c,int resource,int steps) {}
ArrayDeque<State> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
// Track visited by (r,c,resource), not just (r,c).
// For each legal move: update resource and enqueue unseen state.
while(!q.isEmpty()) { State cur=q.poll(); /* expand one BFS layer */ }
```

**GR12 — BFS over graph and subset state.** **When to apply:** Optimal walk must cover every node; visited depends on current vertex and covered subset.

```java
int full=(1<<n)-1;
boolean[][] seen=new boolean[n][1<<n];
ArrayDeque<int[]> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
for(int u=0;u<n;u++){q.add(new int[]{u,1<<u,0});seen[u][1<<u]=true;}
while(!q.isEmpty()) {
    int[] s=q.poll();
    if(s[1]==full) { /* shortest covering walk */ break; }
    for(int v:adj[s[0]]) {int mask=s[1]|(1<<v); if(!seen[v][mask]) {seen[v][mask]=true;q.add(new int[]{v,mask,s[2]+1});}}
}
```

**GR13 — Dijkstra additive nonnegative costs.** **When to apply:** Edge weights are nonnegative; repeatedly finalize the lowest tentative distance using a min-heap.

```java
record Node(int u,long d) {}
long[] dist=new long[n]; Arrays.fill(dist,Long.MAX_VALUE);
PriorityQueue<Node> pq=new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingLong(Node::d));
dist[source]=0;pq.add(new Node(source,0));
while(!pq.isEmpty()) {
    Node cur=pq.poll();if(cur.d()!=dist[cur.u()])continue;
    for(Edge e:adj[cur.u()]) if(cur.d()+e.weight()<dist[e.to()]) {
        dist[e.to()]=cur.d()+e.weight();pq.add(new Node(e.to(),dist[e.to()]));
    }
} // Edge has methods to() and weight().
```

**GR14 — Dijkstra with minimax/path bottleneck.** **When to apply:** Path cost is the maximum edge cost, not a sum; relaxation uses max(pathCost,edgeCost).

```java
record Cell(int id,int cost) {}
int[] best=new int[n]; Arrays.fill(best,Integer.MAX_VALUE);
PriorityQueue<Cell> pq=new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(Cell::cost));
best[start]=0;pq.add(new Cell(start,0));
while(!pq.isEmpty()) {
    Cell cur=pq.poll(); if(cur.cost()!=best[cur.id()])continue;
    for(Edge e:adj[cur.id()]) {
        int candidate=Math.max(cur.cost(),e.weight());
        if(candidate<best[e.to()]) {best[e.to()]=candidate;pq.add(new Cell(e.to(),candidate));}
    }
}
```

**GR15 — 0–1 BFS with deque.** **When to apply:** All edge weights are exactly zero or one; deque pushes free edges to front and costly ones to back.

```java
int[] dist=new int[n]; Arrays.fill(dist,Integer.MAX_VALUE);
ArrayDeque<Integer> dq=new ArrayDeque<>();dist[start]=0;dq.addFirst(start);
while(!dq.isEmpty()) {
    int u=dq.pollFirst();
    for(Edge e:adj[u]) if(dist[u]+e.weight()<dist[e.to()]) {
        dist[e.to()]=dist[u]+e.weight();
        if(e.weight()==0)dq.addFirst(e.to());else dq.addLast(e.to());
    }
} // Store distance with queue entries or tolerate repeated relaxations.
```

**GR16 — Bounded-edge shortest paths / Bellman–Ford.** **When to apply:** A bound on number of edges means ordinary Dijkstra state by vertex alone is insufficient.

```java
long[] prev=new long[n];Arrays.fill(prev,INF);prev[source]=0;
for(int used=1;used<=maxEdges;used++) {
    long[] next=prev.clone();
    for(int[] e:edges) if(prev[e[0]]!=INF)
        next[e[1]]=Math.min(next[e[1]],prev[e[0]]+e[2]);
    prev=next;
} // Each round uses the previous layer only.
```

**GR17 — All-pairs shortest paths / transitive costs.** **When to apply:** Many source-destination comparisons on a small dense graph justify Floyd–Warshall.

```java
long[][] d=new long[n][n];
for(int i=0;i<n;i++){Arrays.fill(d[i],INF);d[i][i]=0;}
for(int[] e:edges)d[e[0]][e[1]]=Math.min(d[e[0]][e[1]],e[2]);
for(int k=0;k<n;k++)for(int i=0;i<n;i++)for(int j=0;j<n;j++)
    if(d[i][k]!=INF && d[k][j]!=INF)
        d[i][j]=Math.min(d[i][j],d[i][k]+d[k][j]);
```

**GR18 — Minimum spanning tree with Prim.** **When to apply:** Connect every vertex at minimum total undirected edge cost, not shortest paths from a source.

```java
boolean[] used=new boolean[n];
PriorityQueue<long[]> pq=new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingLong(a->a[0]));
pq.offer(new long[]{0,0});long total=0;int taken=0;
while(!pq.isEmpty()) {
    long[] item=pq.poll();int u=(int)item[1]; if(used[u])continue;
    used[u]=true;total+=item[0];taken++;
    for(Edge e:adj[u]) if(!used[e.to()])pq.offer(new long[]{e.weight(),e.to()});
} // Success iff taken == n.
```

**GR19 — Bridge detection using Tarjan low-link DFS.** **When to apply:** Need edges whose removal disconnects an undirected graph; compare child low-link to parent discovery time.

```java
int[] tin=new int[n],low=new int[n];Arrays.fill(tin,-1);
// DFS(u,parentEdgeId): tin[u]=low[u]=timer++;
// For each edge(u,v): skip exact parent edge ID;
// if unseen v: DFS(v); low[u]=min(low[u],low[v]);
// if low[v]>tin[u], (u,v) is a bridge;
// else low[u]=min(low[u],tin[v]).
```

**GR20 — Eulerian trail via Hierholzer.** **When to apply:** Use every directed edge exactly once; consume outgoing edges and append vertices after exhausting successors.

```java
Map<Integer,Deque<Integer>> adj=new HashMap<>();
Deque<Integer> stack=new ArrayDeque<>();List<Integer> reversed=new ArrayList<>();
stack.push(start);
while(!stack.isEmpty()) {
    int u=stack.peek();Deque<Integer> out=adj.get(u);
    if(out!=null && !out.isEmpty())stack.push(out.removeFirst());
    else reversed.add(stack.pop());
}
Collections.reverse(reversed); // Euler route if degree/connectivity requirements hold.
```

**GR21 — Topological DAG ancestor propagation.** **When to apply:** Prerequisite ancestry flows forward in DAG order; maintain and merge predecessor sets.

```java
BitSet[] ancestors=new BitSet[n];
for(int i=0;i<n;i++)ancestors[i]=new BitSet(n);
// Obtain Kahn order first.
for(int u:topologicalOrder)for(int v:adj[u]) {
    ancestors[v].or(ancestors[u]);ancestors[v].set(u);
}
```

**GR23 — Leaf peeling for tree centers.** **When to apply:** Repeatedly delete all current leaves; remaining one or two vertices minimize maximum distance.

```java
int[] degree=new int[n];ArrayDeque<Integer> leaves=new ArrayDeque<>();
for(int u=0;u<n;u++){degree[u]=adj[u].size();if(degree[u]<=1)leaves.add(u);}
int remaining=n;
while(remaining>2){int size=leaves.size();remaining-=size;
    for(int i=0;i<size;i++){int u=leaves.poll();for(int v:adj[u])if(--degree[v]==1)leaves.add(v);}
} // leftovers are tree centers.
```

**GR24 — Maximum reachable nodes in a directed reachability graph.** **When to apply:** Build edges from a domain-specific reachability predicate, then explore transitive closures.

```java
List<Integer>[] adj=new List[n];for(int i=0;i<n;i++)adj[i]=new ArrayList<>();
for(int i=0;i<n;i++)for(int j=0;j<n;j++)if(i!=j && canReach(i,j))adj[i].add(j);
for(int start=0;start<n;start++) {
    boolean[] seen=new boolean[n];ArrayDeque<Integer> q=new ArrayDeque<>();
    seen[start]=true;q.add(start);int count=0;
    while(!q.isEmpty()){int u=q.poll();count++;for(int v:adj[u])if(!seen[v]){seen[v]=true;q.add(v);}}
    // maximize reachable count
}
```

**GR25 — Mixed shortest paths and monotone DAG counting.** **When to apply:** Shortest distances impose an acyclic direction; count paths that strictly decrease those distances.

```java
// Run Dijkstra from destination to obtain dist[u].
long[] ways=new long[n];ways[destination]=1;
// Process nodes in ascending distance, or memoize DFS on strictly decreasing distances.
for(int u:orderByIncreasingDistance)for(int v:adj[u])
    if(dist[v]<dist[u])ways[u]=(ways[u]+ways[v])%MOD;
```

O(mnα(mn)). **Recognition:** Adding an undirected same-character neighbor edge creates a cycle if both endpoints are already connected. | BASE: flatten (row,col) to one ID, union equal-character adjacent cells, detect a repeated-root edge. |
| **DS8 — Grid component sizing and reverse activation: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(n²α(n²)). **Recognition:** Cell activation makes connectivity monotone; DSU supports near-constant-time merging and component sizes. | BASE: union present land, evaluate each empty cell using distinct roots and size metadata. |
| 16 | **803** | **DS8**; Bricks falling after sequential hits; remove all hits then restore in reverse, use ceiling sentinel and component-size delta. O((mn+h)α(mn)). **Recognition:** Cell activation makes connectivity monotone; DSU supports near-constant-time merging and component sizes. | Switch one hypothetical activation to multiple reverse-time activations, add ceiling sentinel and record growth excluding restored brick. |
| **DS9 — Two-role edge allocation: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(mα(n)). **Recognition:** Different users share some usable edges; shared connectivity must be prioritized before user-specific edges. | BASE: two DSU instances; count useful edges for each and subtract from total, validate both fully connected. |
| **DS10 — Offline threshold connectivity: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O((m+q)log(m+q)). **Recognition:** Queries ask if a path exists using only edges below a varying weight threshold; sort edges and queries. | BASE: advance one sorted edge cursor while processing queries and restore original query order. |
| **DS11 — Kruskal edge classification: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(m² α(n)) after sorting. **Recognition:** MST edge criticality is tested by excluding or forcing each edge and recomputing a minimal spanning tree. | BASE: index-preserving weighted edges, reusable Kruskal(skip,force), compare MST weights and connectedness. |
| **DS12 — Arithmetic equivalence under divisors: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(n log n α(n)+qα(n)). **Recognition:** Connectivity is induced by a mathematical relation, without explicitly listing every possible edge. | BASE: sieve-like multiple unions replace explicit graph edges; queries are ordinary DSU root tests. |
| **DS13 — Factor-node connectivity: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

| 22 | **1998** | **DS13**; Decide if array can be sorted using swaps with gcd>1; union each number with its prime-factor nodes and compare roots of nums[i] vs sorted[i]. **Recognition:** Two values are connected through shared prime factors; union numbers or their prime factors. | Reuse prime-factor-based components; change objective from largest size to positional feasibility after sorting. |
| **DS14 — Greedy swaps among fixed-position partners: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(nα(n)). **Recognition:** Pairs of entities must be placed together; each successful swap can merge two mismatched pair components. | BASE: collapse identities to couple IDs; each merge reduces disconnected groups by one. |
| **DS15 — Value-ordered DSU activation: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||
| 24 | **2421** | **DS15**; Count good paths by sorted node values; union with neighbors whose values≤current, count current-value nodes per component and add c(c+1)/2. O((n+m)α(n)+n log n). **Recognition:** A path qualifies when all vertices on it have values at most the endpoint value; activate equal-valued vertices in batches. | BASE: sweep node values ascending, union eligible edges, aggregate same-value root frequencies. |
| **DS16 — Reverse deletion of array segments: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

O(nα(n)). **Recognition:** Forward deletions split components; reverse additions only merge components and are DSU-friendly. | BASE: reverse processing, active flags, root sum metadata, write answer before restoring each index. |
| **DS17 — Reverse-time grid connectivity: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

| **DS20 — Same-value component batching with rank propagation: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

| **DS21 — Optimization within swap-connected components: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

| **DS22 — Contagion containment by component metadata: NEW ALGORITHM FAMILY** ||||

### T23 recognition clues and BASIC Java 21 starter templates

**Shared DSU skeleton (conceptual template, not a complete LeetCode solution):**

```java
static final class DSU {
    final int[] parent, size;
    int components;
    DSU(int n) {
        parent = new int[n]; size = new int[n]; components = n;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { parent[i] = i; size[i] = 1; }
    }
    int find(int x) {
        if (parent[x] != x) parent[x] = find(parent[x]);
        return parent[x];
    }
    boolean union(int a, int b) {
        a = find(a); b = find(b);
        if (a == b) return false;
        if (size[a] < size[b]) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }
        parent[b] = a; size[a] += size[b]; components--;
        return true;
    }
}
```

**Invariant:** Every connected component has exactly one representative; `find()` identifies it, `union()` merges only different representatives. Amortized cost is O(α(n)) per operation with both path compression and union by size. Templates below illustrate the *family-specific change*; they intentionally omit problem-specific parsing/output and reuse the skeleton above.

**DS1 — Connectivity and component statistics.** **When to apply:** Repeated undirected connectivity or component-size queries: union each edge and examine final roots.

```java
int[] parent = new int[n], size = new int[n];
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { parent[i] = i; size[i] = 1; }
// For each undirected edge (u,v): if (union(u,v)) components--;
// find(x): compress parents; union: attach smaller root to larger root.
```

**DS2 — Redundant directed/undirected edge detection.** **When to apply:** An edge can be rejected when endpoints already share a root; directed trees also require indegree constraints.

```java
for (int[] e : edges) {
    if (!union(e[0], e[1])) { /* existing path: redundant edge */ }
}
// For directed edges also check whether a child already has a parent.
```

**DS3 — Unify objects by shared identity.** **When to apply:** Different records refer to one entity when a matching key or permitted relation implies equivalence.

```java
Map<String, Integer> owner = new HashMap<>();
for (int i = 0; i < records.size(); i++)
    for (String key : keys(records.get(i))) {
        Integer prev = owner.putIfAbsent(key, i);
        if (prev != null) union(prev, i);
    }
// Group records by find(i).
```

**DS4 — Equality constraints and canonical representatives.** **When to apply:** Constraints create equivalence classes; inequalities or canonical ordering act on those classes.

```java
for (String rule : constraints)
    if (isEquality(rule)) union(left(rule), right(rule));
for (String rule : constraints)
    if (isInequality(rule) && find(left(rule)) == find(right(rule)))
        return false;
```

**DS5 — Bipartite coordinate compression with DSU.** **When to apply:** Rows and columns form a bipartite graph; stones in the same connected component can be removed until one remains.

```java
// Keep row IDs distinct from column IDs using compression or offsets.
for (int[] stone : stones) union(rowId(stone[0]), colId(stone[1]));
// Count roots only among vertices that occur in the input.
```

**DS6 — Subcell topology modeling.** **When to apply:** Visual cell separators alter connectivity inside each grid cell; model subregions explicitly.

```java
int id = 4 * (r * n + c);
// Four triangles: top=0, right=1, bottom=2, left=3.
// Union unseparated triangles inside each cell; union across adjacent cells.
```

**DS7 — Online cycle detection in a grid.** **When to apply:** Adding an undirected same-character neighbor edge creates a cycle if both endpoints are already connected.

```java
for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++)
    for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
        // Check only right/down neighbors with identical characters.
        // A union returning false means a cycle already exists.
    }
```

**DS8 — Grid component sizing and reverse activation.** **When to apply:** Cell activation makes connectivity monotone; DSU supports near-constant-time merging and component sizes.

```java
boolean[] active = new boolean[rows * cols];
// Activate one land cell, then union all active 4-direction neighbors.
// Query size[find(cell)] or a designated ceiling sentinel.
```

**DS9 — Two-role edge allocation.** **When to apply:** Different users share some usable edges; shared connectivity must be prioritized before user-specific edges.

```java
DSU alice = new DSU(n), bob = new DSU(n);
// Process shared type-3 edges in both DSUs first.
// Then consume type-1 in alice, type-2 in bob; count useful edges.
```

**DS10 — Offline threshold connectivity.** **When to apply:** Queries ask if a path exists using only edges below a varying weight threshold; sort edges and queries.

```java
Arrays.sort(edges, Comparator.comparingInt(e -> e[2]));
// Sort indexed queries by their strict weight limits.
int e = 0;
for (Query q : sortedQueries) {
    while (e < edges.length && edges[e][2] < q.limit())
        union(edges[e][0], edges[e++][1]);
    answer[q.index()] = find(q.u()) == find(q.v());
}
```

**DS11 — Kruskal edge classification.** **When to apply:** MST edge criticality is tested by excluding or forcing each edge and recomputing a minimal spanning tree.

```java
long mstCost(int skip, int force) {
    DSU dsu = new DSU(n);
    long total = 0;
    // Force indexed edge if requested; then scan sorted edges except skip/force.
    // Return INF if fewer than n-1 successful unions.
    return total;
}
```

**DS12 — Arithmetic equivalence under divisors.** **When to apply:** Connectivity is induced by a mathematical relation, without explicitly listing every possible edge.

```java
for (int divisor = threshold + 1; divisor <= n; divisor++)
    for (int multiple = divisor * 2; multiple <= n; multiple += divisor)
        union(divisor, multiple);
```

**DS13 — Factor-node connectivity.** **When to apply:** Two values are connected through shared prime factors; union numbers or their prime factors.

```java
Map<Integer, Integer> firstIndexByPrime = new HashMap<>();
for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++)
    for (int p : distinctPrimeFactors(a[i])) {
        Integer prev = firstIndexByPrime.putIfAbsent(p, i);
        if (prev != null) union(prev, i);
    }
```

**DS14 — Greedy swaps among fixed-position partners.** **When to apply:** Pairs of entities must be placed together; each successful swap can merge two mismatched pair components.

```java
int couples = row.length / 2;
for (int i = 0; i < row.length; i += 2)
    union(row[i] / 2, row[i + 1] / 2);
// Minimum swaps = couples - connectedComponentCount.
```

**DS15 — Value-ordered DSU activation.** **When to apply:** A path qualifies when all vertices on it have values at most the endpoint value; activate equal-valued vertices in batches.

```java
// Process vertices by nondecreasing value, grouping equal values.
for (int v : currentValueBatch) {
    for (int u : neighbors[v]) if (value[u] <= value[v]) union(u, v);
}
// Count vertices of this value per root, add c*(c+1)/2.
```

**DS16 — Reverse deletion of array segments.** **When to apply:** Forward deletions split components; reverse additions only merge components and are DSU-friendly.

```java
boolean[] active = new boolean[n];
long[] sum = new long[n];
for (int k = n - 1; k >= 0; k--) {
    answer[k] = best;
    int i = removals[k]; active[i] = true; sum[i] = nums[i];
    // Union active neighbors; root sum accumulates; update best.
}
```

**DS17 — Reverse-time grid connectivity.** **When to apply:** A monotone flooded grid becomes easier if reversed into land activation until opposite borders connect.

```java
// Reopen flooded cells from the final day backward.
// Union new land with 4-direction neighbors and top/bottom sentinels.
// First reversed index with connected sentinels gives the latest day.
```

**DS18 — Time-batched ephemeral connectivity.** **When to apply:** Connections within one timestamp transmit information simultaneously; components must not leak across timestamps.

```java
// Sort meetings by time. Union all edges at timestamp t.
// For all attendees at time t: if find(person) != find(0),
// reset that attendee to a singleton before next timestamp.
```

**DS19 — Union operations with incompatibility constraints.** **When to apply:** Union is permitted only if merging the two current groups violates no restricted pair.

```java
boolean allowed(int u, int v, int[][] restrictions) {
    int ru = find(u), rv = find(v);
    for (int[] r : restrictions) {
        int a = find(r[0]), b = find(r[1]);
        if ((a == ru && b == rv) || (a == rv && b == ru)) return false;
    }
    return true;
}
```

**DS20 — Same-value component batching with rank propagation.** **When to apply:** Equal-valued matrix cells in connected rows/columns need equal rank without conflating other values.

```java
// For each equal-value batch:
// (1) temporary union of cells sharing row or column,
// (2) rank = 1 + max(previousRowRank, previousColRank) per component,
// (3) apply all row/column rank updates simultaneously.
```

**DS21 — Optimization within swap-connected components.** **When to apply:** Allowed swaps permute entries within each DSU group; count multiset overlap with target rather than simulate swaps.

```java
Map<Integer, Map<Integer, Integer>> counts = new HashMap<>();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
    counts.computeIfAbsent(find(i), k -> new HashMap<>())
          .merge(source[i], 1, Integer::sum);
// Consume matching target values only from their own component.
```

**DS22 — Contagion containment by component metadata.** **When to apply:** One initially infected vertex can infect its whole connected component; saving a component requires exactly one initial infection.

```java
int[] infectedPerRoot = new int[n];
for (int x : initiallyInfected) infectedPerRoot[find(x)]++;
// Among roots infected exactly once, maximize root size;
// if tied, prefer smallest initially infected vertex.
```

### T23 completeness and ownership audit

| Essential concept | Coverage / ownership |
|---|---|
| DSU initialization, path compression (recursive or iterative), union by size/rank, inverse Ackermann amortized analysis | T23 shared skeleton + DS1 |
| Undirected components, edge redundancy, component sizes, pair counts, completeness | DS1–DS2 |
| Equivalence classes, canonical keys, string grouping, swap connectivity | DS3–DS4, DS21 |
| Row/column bipartite DSU, triangle subdivision, grid cycle detection | DS5–DS7 |
| Grid activation and reverse-removal connectivity, sentinel supernodes | DS8, DS17 |
| Two independent users, overlapping edge classes | DS9 |
| Offline threshold connectivity and original query ordering | DS10 |
| Kruskal minimum spanning trees, edge critical/pseudocritical classification | DS11 (LC 1489); conventional Prim MST already owned by T22 (LC 1584) |
| Connectivity by divisors and prime factors | DS12–DS13 |
| Swap groups, value-gated activation, maximum sum components | DS14–DS16 |
| Timestamp-ephemeral links and restricted unions | DS18–DS19 |
| Same-value local DSU + rank dependency | DS20 |
| Epidemic spread component metadata | DS22 |
| Rollback/persistent DSU, offline dynamic connectivity with deletions and segment tree over time | T23 companion advanced concepts (no selected LC claimed to directly teach general rollback DSU) |
| Parity/weighted DSU, potential differences, path-compression limitations | T23 companion advanced concepts; ordinary bipartite two-coloring belongs to T22 |
| DSU in grid DFS/BFS tasks already taught as DFS/BFS | T19; LC 200/695/994 remain T19, not duplicated |
| General graph traversal, Dijkstra, Prim, topological sorting | T22; no duplicate questions |
| General segment-tree/range-query infrastructure and offline divide/conquer | T26/T17 respectively; DSU may be composed with these concepts without changing ownership |
| Bitwise Trie, DP, modular arithmetic | T24/T25/T27 respectively |

**Edge cases and engineering requirements:** Map sparse or negative external IDs to dense DSU indices; keep row and column IDs disjoint; avoid recursing over deep parent chains without rank/size balancing; handle isolated vertices and duplicated edges; avoid counting unused sentinel nodes as real components; use `long` for component-pair products, MST totals and segment sums; sort offline queries stably with original indices; do not union different-time batches permanently in LC 2092; do not apply same-value row/column rank updates prematurely in LC 1632.

**Family-boundary audit:** DS1–DS22 denote distinct implementations or materially different core invariants. No connection is claimed between the last problem of one family and the first problem of another. Within DS3, identical root grouping is deliberately reused but the union-generation predicate changes; within DS8, forward single-cell analysis becomes reverse-time repeated activation.

- **Global total:** **710 globally unique locked problems** in T01–T23. T24–T27 remain pending.

## F. Project setup

1. In ChatGPT, manually create one Project called **DSA — Solution-Based Progressive Learning**.
2. Upload this single file, `MASTER_DSA_CURRICULUM.md`, in the Project's **Files** area. Optionally put the global non-negotiable rules into Project instructions too.
3. Inside that Project, create chat `00 — Master Planner` and paste the prompt in Section D.
4. Create chat `T01 — Arrays` and paste the prompt in Section E, replacing `[TOPIC]` with `T01 Arrays`. Type `start` to begin LC 1431.
5. For each new topic, ask the Master Planner to plan and lock its full question sequence **before** creating its practice chat. Download the updated master file, replace the Project file, and then start the topic chat.
6. Keep topic solution exports in your preferred local/Drive folder. ChatGPT's files and your Android file system are not automatically synchronized.
